- **`biome.json` must be strict JSON.** A single `//` comment silently voids the whole config and Biome falls back to defaults — which looks like your rule settings randomly stopped applying. `build/lintConfig.test.ts` fails on a comment and on `useExhaustiveDependencies` dropping below `error`; the seven a11y rules are at `error` too (301 findings were cleared), so keep all of it there.
- **Vitest's environment marker is matched against the WHOLE FILE, not the leading docblock, so writing it out in prose moves that file into jsdom.** `detectCodeBlock` (vitest 4) runs `content.match(/@(?:vitest|jest)-environment\s+([\w-]+)\b/)` over the entire source. A node-only file that so much as quotes the marker — in a string constant, in a comment explaining the convention — is silently switched to the jsdom environment. It bit immediately: `build/domTestNaming.test.ts`, whose whole job is to check that marker, ran under jsdom and died at import on `fileURLToPath`, because `import.meta.url` there is not a `file:` URL. The failure names a line that is obviously fine, which is what makes it expensive. Both the constant and the prose in that file are written with the two halves apart for this reason; keep them apart.
- **A multi-line `biome-ignore` needs `//` on every line.** Biome only honours the directive on the line immediately preceding the offence, so put prose in a normal comment block above a single-line directive. Getting this wrong leaves bare text inside JSX and breaks the parse.
- **DisassemblyView.tsx** is ~1620 lines even after the split (1624 at `488ddde`; it was ~1520 when this was first written). Read in chunks. The two seams that used to live here are now `hooks/useDisassemblyKeyboard.ts` (`handleKeyDown`, ~330 lines with a **38-entry** dependency array — that array *is* the behaviour; copy it verbatim if you move it, and note the entries carrying `//` comments explaining why a stable value is listed or a value is deliberately omitted) and `hooks/useGraphSearch.ts`. That array only began doing anything recently: `useDisassemblySearch` returned a bare object literal, so `search` had a fresh identity every render and the `useCallback` memoised nothing, leaving the other entries inert decoration. It was audited entry-by-entry over the AST before the memo landed and came back exactly 1:1, so nothing latent was exposed; `hooks/__tests__/disasmHandlerDeps.test.ts` now fails the build if it drifts in either direction. `CFGView` now takes 23 props, several of the former ones read from context instead. Both extractions were strictly mechanical, and neither has ever been rendered.
- **A callback declared later in a component cannot go in an earlier hook's dependency array** — it is a `const`, so the array hits its temporal dead zone at hook-call time. This is not hypothetical: `handleKeyDown` closed over `handleDecompileToggle` (declared ~340 lines later) without it in the deps, so **D opened the decompile panel but could not close it** — the memoized handler kept a closure where `showDecompile` was still `false`. The fix is a ref assigned *during render*; an effect is too late, because a keypress can be handled before effects flush.
- **`parseBranchTarget` lives only in `components/shared.tsx`** and resolves `call` immediates as well as jumps. JumpArrows draws jump arrows only, so it guards with `mnemonic.startsWith("j")` *before* calling — dropping that guard makes recursive/intra-function calls sprout arrows. Covered by `src/components/__tests__/parseBranchTarget.test.ts`.
- `sectionInfo.characteristics & 0x20000000` = `IMAGE_SCN_MEM_EXECUTE`. Used to distinguish code vs data sections.
- **A64 mnemonic matching is by *exact* mnemonic, never by prefix.** `brk` is not a `br` and `bfi` is not a branch, so `startsWith("b")` — the habit x86 encourages — mis-classifies real instructions. `arm64Operands.ts` is the one place that knows the grammar (`b`, `b.<cc>`, `bl`, `br`/`blr` including the PAuth forms, `cbz`/`cbnz`, `tbz`/`tbnz`, `ret`, and the `adrp`+`add`/`ldr` address idiom); `buildCFG`, `layoutCFG`, `parseBranchTarget`, the `JumpArrows` guard and `buildTypedXrefMap` all read it rather than re-deriving it. The x86 path is untouched by any of it and its edge and xref lists are byte-identical — keep it that way; the one shared spelling (`ret`) is deliberately left on the x86 path, where both architectures already yield nothing.
- **Don't put a cheap request on the disasm worker.** It services messages serially, so a checksum or entropy call posted behind a whole-image disassembly waits minutes. That is what `metrics.worker.ts` exists for. The same reasoning is why a `useMemo` is not an option: it cannot yield, and on a 253 MiB PE the entropy strip froze the UI for ~6.5 s on every Hex tab open whether or not the strip was ever shown.
- **Never put a caller-owned buffer in a worker transfer list.** Structured clone of an `ArrayBufferView` serialises its whole backing `ArrayBuffer`, not the view's window, and every large byte argument here is a view onto the *entire loaded file* — so a 4 KiB single-function `disassemble` used to copy all 253 MiB. `disasmClient.send()` routes args through `prepareBinaryArgs` (`workers/transfer.ts`), which replaces each top-level binary argument with a private `slice()` of exactly that window and transfers **only buffers it allocated itself**. That invariant is what makes the detach hazard structurally impossible: the main thread keeps reading the file through `bufferRef`, `pe.buffer`, HexView and entropy, and transferring a caller's buffer would detach the loaded file under them. The walk is **top-level only** — an `Instruction[]` carries a tiny `bytes` buffer per element, and transferring 500k of those measured **80.6 s against 1.6 s to clone**. Anything that must cross flattened (e.g. `packDataWindows`) does so for the same reason. Slicing wins even when the copy is the same size (186 → 96 ms), so there is no threshold below which this is skipped.
- **…and the REPLY path was measured, refused, and its instrument LOST — it is landed now, and the recorded table survives re-derivation.** `peek-a-bin-rjt` is the return-path half of the bullet above: the reply to `disassemble`/`hybridDisassemble` is an `Instruction[]`, posted by `disasm.worker.ts` as `self.postMessage({ id, result })` with **no transfer list**, and each element's `bytes` is a view onto its own small private buffer — so there is no whole-buffer amplification on the way back, just N small buffers. The bead refused packing them on a four-row table taken at 500k instructions and ended "Bench: scratchpad bench-reply.mjs"; two staleness audits then confirmed no such file existed, so the table was unreproducible — CLAUDE.md's own `peek-a-bin-02fa` hazard, and its standing lesson **"when you build an oracle to verify a change, land the oracle"**. `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts` is that oracle (`npm run corpus:replycost -- <path>`). **The decision is unchanged; what changed is that it now has a base.** Measured idle at `488ddde`, synthetically at the bead's own 500k:

  | row | recorded | re-derived | agreement |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | A clone as-is (today) | 1640 ms | **1794 ms** | +9% |
  | B transfer every per-insn buffer | 80568 ms | **80128 ms** | **−0.5%** |
  | C pack bytes + transfer | 1085 ms | **1219 ms** | +12% |
  | D no `bytes` field (floor) | 789 ms | **892 ms** | +13% |

  Row B agreeing to within half a percent on a figure of 80 seconds is the strongest evidence available that the lost bench measured what this one measures. Six things:
  - **ROW B IS STRONGLY SUPERLINEAR, WHICH ONE POINT COULD NOT SHOW, and that is a stronger claim than the 80568 ms.** Per-buffer cost rises **5.7 → 164 µs over N = 5000 → 500000**, an exponent of about **1.7**. So the harm from a large transfer list *grows with the image* rather than being a fixed tax, and `prepareBinaryArgs` being top-level only is the difference between a linear reply and a superlinear one. Do not round the exponent to 2 — it is measured and it is between the two. A **linear** extrapolation of B is a lower bound, never an estimate.
  - **C IS NOT THE ROW TO READ; C+ IS.** The bead's refusal is that an unpacked `bytes` is a view onto one shared buffer and those instructions go back a function at a time, so the receiver must re-slice — "which gives back most of the 555 ms". Measured, it does: on a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image (155531 instructions) C saves **103.6 ms of a 463.5 ms reply and C+ saves 92.7 ms**, and on the four corpus binaries C+ saves **4.9–8.6 ms of a ~45 ms reply**. `A − D` — the whole budget any scheme here competes for — is only 208 ms of 464 on the `go` image, because most of a reply is object and string overhead rather than bytes.
  - **THE BEAD'S PREMISE ABOUT PROVENANCE HAS MOVED AND IT DOES NOT MATTER.** The bead says `bytes` is capstone-wasm's `HEAPU8.slice(ptr, ptr+24).subarray(0, size)`; since `peek-a-bin-iqzu`, `gridScan` serves almost everything with an exact-size `.slice()`, so the `go` image is **155531 of 155531 exact-size**, t64/w64 entirely so, and only **214 of t32's 18045** and 196 of w32's 16606 still come from Capstone. Negative control 2 widens every backing buffer back to 24 B and reads a **null result whose sign is not stable between runs** (−17%, −10%, −5%, +17%) — ~20 B of backing per instruction is below this reply's per-object noise floor. So that change did not move the reply's cost in either direction.
  - **`src/disasm/linearSweep.ts`'s COMMENT OVERSTATES ITS OWN CASE BY A FACTOR OF N, and the rule is unaffected.** It says a `subarray` of the section there "would make the reply's structured clone serialise the WHOLE `.text` once per instruction". Measured: **once per MESSAGE.** `StructuredSerializeInternal` carries a memory map, so an `ArrayBuffer` referenced by many views is serialised once and the deserialised views all share one buffer — 500 views of an 8 MiB buffer clone to exactly **1 buffer of 8 MiB** (control 4, a property check rather than a timing). `.slice()` is still right: a whole-section tax on every reply and on every ~100-instruction request back is a real cost, control 3 measures it at 112x the private baseline, and the **send** path is untouched by dedup because there is only one view there anyway — a single 16-byte view onto 8 MiB still drags all 8 MiB.
  - **The return trip is proportional to the PACKED BUFFER, not to the instruction count, which is the dedup finding applied.** `peek-a-bin-9gc9` made a ~100-instruction slice the shape of every decompile request, and a shared packed buffer would be paid **once per request** rather than once per instruction. On these images the packed buffer is under a megabyte and the tax is at the noise floor (2x and 7 ms per 100 opened functions on the `go` image; negative, i.e. noise, on all four corpus binaries). Read `packed KiB`, not the ratio — it grows with the image while the row-C saving it is set against does not grow faster.
  - **TWO OF THE FOUR NEGATIVE CONTROLS CAME BACK INERT AND WERE REBUILT, which is this repo's recurring failure mode recurring.** Control 3 first used a 669 KiB section and read inert; a second version compared against the private clone with a 10x threshold and read **17x idle but 5x while the test suites ran** — a verdict that depended on machine load. It is now judged on the claim being controlled (the tax tracks the *section*, not N) by holding N fixed and growing the section, with a pass condition that is a memcpy of tens of MB against a hundred small objects and therefore load-proof. Control 2 is a genuine null and is **reported rather than tuned until it moves**.

  **NOT VERIFIED, and it is `corpus/decompileRpcCost.ts`' gap exactly**: nothing here spawns a real `Worker`, so `structuredClone` in one process stands in for a `postMessage` across two — it serialises *and* deserialises on one thread, which **over-states** what the main thread blocks for, so every row is an upper bound on what a user feels. No human has watched a reply cross a real `postMessage`. Run it idle: the 500k row allocates several hundred MB and read 6.99 µs/insn for A beside the test suites against 3.59 idle.
- **…and the same region is copied again on every call. UPLOADING IT ONCE UNDER A HANDLE WAS MEASURED AND REFUSED — do not rebuild it without new evidence.** One load ships `.text` four times (`detectFunctions`, `buildAllXrefs`, `hybridDisassemble`, `buildAllXrefs` again when strings arrive) and each pays its own slice; `peek-a-bin-9a8` proposed a client-side handle plus a worker-side `Map`. **The saving is 0.0075%–0.087% of the work done on those same bytes, and the fraction does not improve with size** — measured at `decbea4` through the real `dispatch` over all four corpus binaries, both ARM64 binaries and a 669 KiB-`.text` Windows/amd64 PE from `go` (t32 0.0075%, w64 0.0319%, go x64 0.0367%, t64-arm 0.0746%, w64-arm 0.0868%). A slice costs ~0.4 ms/MiB of section and the decoding it feeds costs 1500–6700 ms/MiB of the *same* section, both linear, so the ratio is a property of the tool and not of the file: at the 200 MiB `.text` the bead was written about it is ~0.23 s out of ~11 minutes, and `hybridDisassemble` alone is then at or past `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, so that image cannot be analysed at all. Five things:
  - **The two ARM64 rows are the hostile case, measured on purpose.** There `Arm64SweepCache` has already removed the Capstone work from two of the three RPCs, so the denominator collapses and the trade is as good as it ever gets. Still under a tenth of one percent — and **`peek-a-bin-x40u` has since put x86 in the same place**, so the hostile case is now the ordinary one: re-measured after it the `go` image reads 0.0357% and t32 0.0072%. **`peek-a-bin-iqzu` then cut the denominator again** (`hybridDisassemble` served from the same held sweep), and re-measured after that it is **0.0547% and 0.0275%** — still under a tenth of one percent after the work has been cut roughly threefold twice over, which is the point: both terms are linear in the section, so the ratio is a property of the tool. An agent arriving with "the work is much smaller now, is it worth it yet" has the answer measured three times, on both architectures: no.
  - **The bead's headline figure describes the cost `peek-a-bin-7mf` already removed.** "116–192 ms of memcpy per send" is the *cloned view* column of `transfer.ts`' table; a send now costs the slice, 64–76 ms at 200 MiB on this machine, matching its `slice + transfer` column. The available saving was always about half what the bead claims.
  - **A STALE HIT CANNOT BE MADE IMPOSSIBLE CHEAPLY, and that is the refusal.** There are two kinds of key. A **content** key — `Arm64SweepCache`'s — is sound against anything including an in-place patch, and costs a full linear pass, *which is the very thing being saved*: a compare is not cheaper than a memcpy, so a sound version saves nothing by construction. An **identity** key — `(ArrayBuffer, byteOffset, byteLength)`, the bead's sketch — is O(1) and is sound only while the file buffer is never written in place. That invariant holds today (audited: the only writers of file-derived bytes are `useDisassemblyRows`' hex patch into a fresh `new Uint8Array(sectionBytes)`, and `HexView`'s download into a `pe.buffer.slice(0)`) and **nothing enforces it** — no type, no test, no lint rule — and the change that breaks it is an obvious one: making hex editing cheap by writing a byte into the buffer instead of copying the section per keystroke. After that every later disassembly silently answers from the pre-patch bytes, and **no corpus gate can see it because none of them drives the worker at all**.
  - **The general rule this settles: the key comparison must be cheaper than the work it saves.** Both in-tree caches pass it and this one fails it. `Arm64SweepCache` spends ~0.4 ms/MiB of byte compare to skip ~1500 ms/MiB of Capstone — four orders of magnitude of margin, which is *why* it can afford the sound key. `CallSummaryCache` spends an O(1) token compare to skip an ~18 ms build, and its token is exact because an `Instruction[]` is minted fresh by a decode. Here the work saved *is* a linear pass over the bytes, so there is no room underneath it for any key at all.
  - **The premise's own detail decides the design and is re-derived by the harness, not asserted**: the three call sites each build their own `new Uint8Array(fileBuffer, pointerToRawData, sizeOfRawData)`, so keying on view *identity* would collapse 2 of the 4 sends and only the triple collapses all four. `npm run corpus:uploadcost -- <path>` is the instrument — a path-taking census outside `npm run corpus` for `jumpTableReach.ts`' reason, printing the work, the copy and the ratio for any PE, with nothing tabulated that can go stale. **Where the leverage was, and it has been taken:** on x86 the section was not merely re-*sent* four times but re-*decoded*, each RPC opening its own `createScan`. `peek-a-bin-x40u` shares that decode; see the entry below. Note the two are independent — sharing the decode leaves this module's copy exactly as it was and makes its *ratio* marginally worse, because the denominator shrank.
- **…and the DECOMPILE RPC's payload was 92-99% of a request, but the fix is NOT residency: the whole array was never READ in full, so it stops crossing rather than starting to live in the worker.** `decompileFunction` shipped the whole section's `Instruction[]` and the whole section's typed xref map on every request — 476-652 ms and 52-65 ms of clone on a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image, against 5-6 ms of decompiling. `peek-a-bin-9gc9` was filed as worker-side *residency* under `insnsToken`, on the premise that the array has to be somewhere; the premise is wrong, and reading the consumers is what shows it. **`decompileFunction` passes the array to `buildCFG` and to nothing else, and `buildCFG` narrows it with `getFuncInsns` on its first line; it passes the map to the same place, which reads it only at `xrefMap.get(insn.address)` for those same instructions.** So the client sends *this function's* slice of each — `collectFuncInsns` and `funcXrefEntries`, one declaration of "belongs to this function", side by side in `funcInsns.ts`. **Measured at 11408ac, both sides pinned, two runs: a request is 16.6-22.9x cheaper on the four corpus binaries and 56-68x on the `go` image** (t32 56.6 → 3.4 ms, t64 54.8 → 3.0, w32 53.0 → 2.3, w64 49.0 → 2.4, go 629 → 11.2), payload share 94.7-99.2% → 11.3-46.9%, and opening every function of the `go` image goes **1242 s → 22.7 s**. Eight things:
  - **RETENTION IS UNCHANGED — ZERO — and that is the whole advantage over the bead's shape.** Residency would have the worker hold a second copy of an array the main thread already holds, ~135 B/instruction, 24 MB for the `go` image (`peek-a-bin-x40u`'s figure), plus a key: a content key costs a linear pass over the very thing being saved (`peek-a-bin-9a8`'s rule) and an identity key does not survive structured clone. Nothing is held here. The `WeakMap` token already existed and is untouched.
  - **The ONE consumer of the whole section is the callee-clobber summary**, which is closed over the call graph and so is a property of the image rather than of this function. It is cached against the token, so it is read on the first request bearing one and never again — which is why the array can stop crossing per request without the summary being lost.
  - **THE WORKER ASKS; THE CLIENT DOES NOT PREDICT.** On a summary miss `dispatch` returns `{ needInstructions: true }` *above* the work and the client resends the same request with the array. A client-side belief was available and is refused twice over: `CallSummaryCache` holds exactly **one** entry and `configure` clears it, so mirroring it would put an eviction rule in two files — the shape `sections.ts`, `ripRelative.ts`, `funcInsns.ts` and `stackIdiom.ts` each exist to end — and the failure mode of a stale belief is the bad one. A request answered *without* a summary it should have had is well-formed C, and the client's decompile cache is keyed on the function's address, so it would be served for the rest of the session: `peek-a-bin-s2ws`'s own named hazard. Bounded at one retry by construction, since a request carrying the array can always build.
  - **The ask costs one round trip per file on x64 and NONE anywhere else, measured rather than argued.** Decompiling every function of all five images: **1 resend on each x64 image, 0 on both PE32 images** (1974 messages for 1973 requests on the `go` image). PE32 needs none because `calleeClobbersFor` returns nothing unless `is64` — and that gate stays in `dispatch.ts` alone, which is what `peek-a-bin-yavq` refused a client-side `funcExtents: is64 ? …` for. (`llm/decompileForLLM.ts`, which sent no extents at all and so never provoked one, was a third caller at the time of the measurement and went at `peek-a-bin-1xc5`. The figures above stand for the callers that remain and have **not** been re-measured.) The first click is not made worse: the extra trip carries a slim payload, ~4 ms against the 556 ms the array costs either way.
  - **EQUIVALENCE IS A CENSUS, AND IT IS 0 OF 3059.** `npm run corpus:decompilecost -- <path>` now decompiles every function twice, once from each payload, through the real `dispatch` under the real protocol: **0 functions of 267/279/265/275/1973 emit different C** on t32/t64/w32/w64/go, in both runs. It re-derives its own premise both ways too (`prepareBinaryArgs` still finds nothing binary at the top level, so `funcInsns`' per-element `bytes` views still cross by clone).
  - **THE XREF HALF IS UNOBSERVABLE ON THIS CORPUS IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, so it rests on the structural argument and a behavioural guard.** Dropping the *whole* xref map changes the emitted C of **not one** of those 3059 functions — every leader it would add, the direct-branch scan already added — and an out-of-window row can never be consulted by construction. The mechanism is nevertheless live: an injected in-range branch xref does move the output (digest `8da871` → `7133a6` on t32). So the guard is `buildCFG consults the xref map only inside the function` in `src/disasm/__tests__/funcInsns.test.ts`, which hands `buildCFG` a `Map` that records every key it is asked for and fails on a stray one — a behavioural check rather than a source scrape, with rows on only every other address so a `get(a) ?? get(a + 4)` fallback is recorded too. Both shapes were negative-controlled; a first draft of that fixture had a row at every address and read clean over an unconditional out-of-range query.
  - **Ten negative controls, and two came back INERT and were fixed rather than kept.** Surgical: a memo that never stores → `does not ask again once the worker holds the summary`; a memo keyed too loosely (`peek` ignoring the token) → `asks for a token it does not hold`; a client that keeps sending the whole section → 2 tests; the whole xref map → 1; the xref window shifted past the function → 7. Broad, as intended: a worker that shrugs and answers without the summary, and a client that does not retry → 6 tests each. The two inert ones are the lesson — a slim window one byte short changed nothing while the fixture's function had a *padded* size, and a `get(a) ?? get(end)` perturbation changed nothing while every address had a row. Both fixtures were sharpened until the control bit (3 tests and 1 test respectively). A control that does not discriminate is a test that is not testing.
  - **THE RESIDUE MOVES `peek-a-bin-yavq`'s ARITHMETIC and a future agent should know it.** With the two big arrays gone, the `go` image's remaining 4.65 ms payload is **`funcEntries` 2.18 ms (19.5% of a request), `runtimeFunctions` 1.53 ms (13.7%) and `funcExtents` 0.645 ms (5.8%)**, against `funcInsns` at 0.012 ms and the xref rows at 0.004 ms. `funcEntries` cannot be cached — it carries renames. `runtimeFunctions` is the `.pdata` array, invariant under renames and a property of the image, and was the second-largest member; **`peek-a-bin-qmlz` has since taken it — see the next bullet — and it needed no protocol at all.** And `funcExtents` — refused at 0.1-0.2% of a request — is now ~6% of a much smaller one, with the "I do not hold that token, resend" protocol yavq called "not a one-liner" now built and in use. The **absolute** is unchanged, which is what that refusal actually rested on, so this is a reason to re-measure and not a reason to assume.

  **NOT VERIFIED, and it is the same gap `peek-a-bin-s2ws` records**: nothing here renders a component or spawns a real `Worker`, so `structuredClone` in one process stands in for a `postMessage` across two — which over-states main-thread blocking, making every figure above an upper bound on what a user feels rather than a measurement of it. **The two-message protocol has never crossed a real `postMessage`**, only the real client answered by the real `dispatch` in `src/workers/__tests__/disasmClient.test.ts`. `npm run corpus` is the control and is byte-identical — it drives `FileSession`, which has no worker — verified rather than assumed: `diff -r` over the whole artifact tree finds only the label line and the artifact path, `compare.mjs` "byte-identical throughout" on all four binaries with `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0` and verdict "no regression"; `npm run corpus:arm64` 51/51 green.
- **…and the LARGEST REMAINING member of that payload was the `.pdata` table, which is read to pick AT MOST ONE ROW.** With the two big arrays gone, `runtimeFunctions` was the second-largest member of a decompile request and the largest one that can be cut at all — `funcEntries` above it carries renames — at **1.33-1.39 ms on a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image (1641 rows), 19% of a request and 39% of what was left of the payload**, plus 0.19-0.22 ms on t64 (240 rows) and 0.21 ms on w64 (235). Reading the consumer is again the whole method (`peek-a-bin-9gc9`'s lesson applied to `peek-a-bin-qmlz`): `decompileFunction` hands the array to `wrapExceptionRegions` and to nothing else, and that picks **one record or none**, so the client applies the same rule and sends the survivor. **Measured at `755ea94`, with the table and the row clocked in the SAME process so machine noise cancels: 1.369 → 0.001 ms (`go`), 0.193 → 0.001 (t64), 0.210 → 0.003 (w64)**, i.e. the member's share goes 18.94% → 0.012%, 7.24% → 0.032% and 8.69% → 0.150%, and the whole per-function payload **4.162 → 2.599 ms, 0.670 → 0.419 and 1.137 → 0.647**. Two runs, both sides pinned. Seven things:
  - **THE PREDICATE IS A BEGIN-ADDRESS EQUALITY, NOT THE WINDOW ITS TWO NEIGHBOURS USE AND NOT AN EXTENT INTERSECTION** — which is the thing to get right, because a `.pdata` row *has* an extent and the intersection reading looks like the natural one. `wrapExceptionRegions` matches `rf.beginAddress` against `func.address` and consults the extent only as a tie-break between rows that already begin at the same place; a row covering the function without beginning at it describes some other function's frame. `funcExceptionRecord` (`disasm/funcInsns.ts`) is the one declaration, and both the containment and the intersection readings are negative-controlled against it.
  - **THE UNITS ARE THE TRAP, and the naive slice is `peek-a-bin-yrh` reintroduced one file earlier.** `.pdata` holds RVAs and `DisasmFunction.address` is a VA, so `[func.address, func.address + func.size)` applied to a row matches **nothing** on any real x64 image — silently, since the C still compiles and merely stops saying the function has a handler. The rule therefore recovers the image base from the pair (a VA and its RVA differ by a multiple of 64K) and **discards an ambiguous match** rather than guessing, exactly as it did inside the pipeline.
  - **NO PROTOCOL, NO KEY, NO CACHE — the rule is applied on BOTH sides and it is IDEMPOTENT.** Handed `[rf]` it returns `rf`, whichever of its two branches selected it, so the worker re-deriving the answer from the one row it was sent gets the same answer as from the table. That is what makes the slice exact rather than a client-side prediction, and it is why `peek-a-bin-9a8`'s rule ("the key comparison must be cheaper than the work it saves") does not even arise: there is no key. A caller sending the whole table — the MCP server, `corpus/sweep.ts`, and at the time of this measurement `llm/decompileForLLM.ts`, since removed at `peek-a-bin-1xc5` — is unchanged by construction.
  - **The rule had to move to a LEAF, and that is why it is in `funcInsns.ts` rather than staying in `pipeline.ts`.** `disasmClient` cannot import the decompile pipeline: that would pull the emitter, the structurer and struct synthesis onto the main thread. `funcInsns.ts` imports nothing but types, and the two members already there answer the same shape of question.
  - **PE32 HAS NO `.pdata` AT ALL** (0 rows on t32 and w32 against 240/235 on t64/w64), so this is an x64 saving and both 32-bit binaries are the byte-identical control. Their member *share* rises — 0.002% → ~0.03% — purely because the denominator shrank, which is the same trap `peek-a-bin-yavq`'s `funcExtents` share sets; the absolute is 0.001 ms either way.
  - **THE CENSUS NEEDED A LIVENESS HALF, because `0 differing` is a statement about a population that can be empty.** `runtimeFunctions` is read at one place and its whole observable effect is a `__try`, so an image emitting none would score a change that dropped the array entirely as clean. `corpus/decompileRpcCost.ts` now prints the row count, how many rows the per-function payload sends, and the `__try` count under each payload: **50 on t64 and 46 on w64, equal both ways** — which are also exactly the handler-bearing row counts, so the emitted C and the table corroborate each other — 2 on the `go` image, and **0 on the PE32 pair, labelled vacuous rather than reported as a pass**. Equivalence itself is **0 of 3059 functions** emitting different C, in each of two runs.
  - **Ten negative controls, and ONE CAME BACK INERT and the fixture was sharpened rather than the control kept.** Swapping the begin-address equality for a containment test passed all 32 tests, because the fixture had the record in RVAs and the function at a real VA — so containment failed on the *offset* rather than on the rule. A same-unit fixture (0x1010 against 0x1000..0x2000) is what makes it bite, and the intersection reading with it. The others: dropped handler filter, dropped ambiguity discard, dropped extent tie-break, dropped same-address preference, a window slice in place of the base recovery, a client sending the whole table, a client sending `table()[0]`, and a client slicing by window. This is the repo's recurring failure mode and it recurred; `9gc9` reported two inert controls in the same place.

  **NOT VERIFIED, the same gap as `9gc9`**: nothing renders a component or spawns a real `Worker`, so `structuredClone` in one process stands in for a `postMessage` across two and every figure is an upper bound on what a user feels. `npm run corpus` is the control and is byte-identical — `diff -r` over the whole artifact tree finds only the label line and the artifact path, verdict "no regression", `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0` and "byte-identical throughout" on all four binaries, with t64's 50 and w64's 46 `__try`s intact — and it exercises the *refactor* rather than the slice, since `corpus/sweep.ts` still passes the whole table. `npm run corpus:arm64` 51/51 with the differential still 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131. **The ARM64 shape is untested in anger**: an ARM64 `RuntimeFunction` carries a nested `arm64Frame`, so a slice there would save more, but the decompiler refuses A64 above this and no ARM64 image reaches the rule.
- **…and THE SAME ARRAY GOES BACK *UP* ONE MESSAGE LATER, where the consumer reads four fields of it and never a byte.** `buildTypedXrefMap` is the one RPC that sends a decoded `Instruction[]` *up* to the worker after the worker produced it — `useDisassemblyRows` takes the array out of `hybridDisassemble` and hands it straight back — and `prepareBinaryArgs` walks **top level only**, so `instructions` being an array means every element's `bytes` is structured-cloned as **its own `ArrayBuffer`**. Each of those is a private per-instruction buffer by the `capstoneReader.ts`/`gridScan` rules — on this tree all of them exact-size `gridScan` slices, `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts` reporting `16844 exact-size, 0 24-byte` on t64, which is the provenance shift that entry already records — and the per-buffer overhead is the whole cost of the array rather than the bytes in it. The consumer reads `address`, `mnemonic`, `opStr` and — through `resolveRipTarget` — `size`. It now receives exactly those, as `XrefInsn`. Eight things (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.3`):
  - **THIS IS NEITHER `peek-a-bin-7mf`'s NOR `peek-a-bin-9a8`'s REFUSED CHANGE, and a future agent reading only the diff will assume it is one of them.** `7mf` refused **reply packing**: the *down* direction, where the objection is that an unpacked shared buffer forces the receiver to re-slice a function at a time (row C+ in `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts`, which is why C is not the row to read). `9a8` refused **uploading the section once under a handle**: the objection there is that no key is both cheap and sound — a content key costs the linear pass being saved, an identity key rests on an invariant nothing enforces — so a stale hit cannot be made impossible. This is the *up* direction, it packs nothing, and it has **no key at all**: nothing is remembered, so nothing can go stale, and the receiver gets whole objects it does not have to unpack. It is `9gc9`'s own rule applied where `9gc9` said to apply it — *where a payload is worth cutting, the fix is to send only what the consumer reads.*
  - **THE DELIVERABLE IS A TYPE, NOT A `.map()` IN THE CLIENT.** `XrefInsn` sits in `functionDetect.ts` beside `ImageBounds`, on `RipInsn`'s model ("the minimum an instruction has to expose"): the **consumer's own declared parameter type**. `Instruction` satisfies it structurally, so `mcp/disasm.ts`, `mcp/session.ts`, `dispatch.ts`, `corpus/`'s callers and every existing test compile unchanged and no direct caller pays anything. A hand-written projection in the client alone would be a **second declaration of what the consumer reads**, free to drift from the consumer silently and in the one direction — worker-side `undefined` — that produces well-formed wrong answers rather than a crash. The strip is therefore annotated `XrefInsn[]` and **never inferred**, which closes the loop in *both* directions, each measured rather than argued: a `bytes` read added inside `buildTypedXrefMap` fails to compile (`error TS2339: Property 'bytes' does not exist on type 'XrefInsn'`), and a field added to `XrefInsn` fails to compile **at the strip**, as a missing property, rather than being posted short.
  - **`size` IS WHAT MAKES THE NARROWING EXACT RATHER THAN LUCKY, AND IT IS INVISIBLE TO THE OBVIOUS GREP.** `insn.` over the whole function body returns `address`, `mnemonic`, `opStr` — three fields, and a narrowing built from that reading would be wrong. `size` is read *indirectly*, by `resolveRipTarget`, because a RIP displacement is `address + size + disp`. Dropping it is the control that matters and it discriminates twice over: the client-side equivalence differential's `[rip + 0x100]` entry goes `5368713484` → **`NaN`**, and both narrowed rows in `functionDetect.test.ts` redden; and removing `size` from `XrefInsn` altogether **fails to typecheck inside `functionDetect.ts`** — `Argument of type 'XrefInsn' is not assignable to parameter of type 'RipInsn'` — so the type system refuses an inexact narrowing with no test consulted at all. That last is the tie-back to `ripRelative.ts`: `RipInsn` is what makes `XrefInsn` unable to under-declare.
  - **THE EQUIVALENCE DIFFERENTIAL IS NOT A CONTROL FOR THE STRIP, and mistaking it for one would be the whole error.** `answers identically to the same request carrying bytes` drives the **real** client and answers it with the **real** `dispatch` twice — once from the payload the worker actually received, once from the full `Instruction[]` — and compares the maps. Under control 1 (send the array un-stripped) it is **green**, which is exactly its point: it says the strip is *harmless*, never that it happened. What says the strip happened is the key-set row, and what says it is exact is the `size` control above. It carries a liveness half for the reason `armExits` and `parserdiff` do — an empty map compares equal to an empty map — so it pins one xref per classifying arm (direct call, RIP displacement, bounded fallback scan) and a length of 3.
  - **Three controls, all discriminating, none inert.** (1) Send the un-stripped array → the key-set row and `leaves the caller's own instructions untouched` redden, the equivalence row stays green, as predicted. (2) Strip `size` too → the key-set row and the equivalence row redden (`NaN`); the two `functionDetect.test.ts` rows redden. (3) Add a `bytes` read to the consumer → typecheck fails, 1 error. The key-set assertion is an **exact key set**, not `!("bytes" in el)`, so it also pins `comment` and `source` out and fails if a field reaches the wire without `XrefInsn` declaring it.
  - **ONE PRE-EXISTING TEST WAS RETARGETED RATHER THAN WEAKENED, and which way round that goes is the point.** `does not transfer the tiny per-instruction buffers in an Instruction[]` asserted `worker.received[0].args.instructions[0].bytes.byteLength === 4` — a claim about `prepareBinaryArgs`' top-level-only walk that happened to use `buildTypedXrefMap` as its vehicle. After the strip there is no `bytes` on that wire to leave out of a transfer list, so the row would have had to assert something weaker about the same RPC. It is asked of **`detectIRPDispatches`** instead, which still sends the whole decoded array, so the transfer-list claim survives intact and at full strength.
  - **`npm run corpus` is byte-identical BY CONSTRUCTION, which is a weaker statement than it looks and is made deliberately.** That run goes through `FileSession`, which calls `buildTypedXrefMap` **directly** and never through the worker, so no corpus binary's emitted C could move whatever this change did — the four `emitted C identical` lines are a check that the refactor did not disturb the direct callers, not evidence that the strip is sound. The evidence for the strip is the equivalence differential plus the type. Same standing as `x40u`'s corpus control, and for the same reason.

  - **THE SIZE OF THE SAVING IS `A − D`, AND IT IS QUOTED FROM AN EXISTING HARNESS RATHER THAN TIMED AGAIN.** `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts` already clones the same array with `bytes` (row A) and with the field absent (row D), so `A − D` *is* this change and `D` is what is left on the RPC. Measured at `0870e14` on `t64.exe` (60 KiB `.text`, 279 functions, 16844 instructions): **A 115.2 ms, D 85.1 ms, A − D 30.1 ms**, i.e. this takes **26% off** the clone of that array and leaves 74%. `9gc9`'s 652 ms figure is the same row on a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image, ~11x the section — read the ratio, not the digits, since these are wall clock on a shared machine. **The remaining 74% is precisely stage 3b's case** (compute the map in the `hybridDisassemble` dispatch arm that produced the array and pre-seed the client's `xrefCache`), so 3b's share is ~2.8x this one's; that bead is deliberately left to be decided **with the number in hand**, and the harness now prints this reading and the commit beside the budget line so the next agent does not have to re-derive it.

  **NOT VERIFIED, and it is `9gc9`'s and `qmlz`'s gap unchanged**: nothing here spawns a real `Worker`, so `structuredClone` in one process stands in for a `postMessage` across two — an upper bound on what a user feels, and every figure above inherits that. Nothing was separately timed on *this* tree: the change is a payload narrowing whose size is a property of the payload, so the harness's own rows are the measurement and re-running it is the way to re-check it.
- **…AND THEN THE ROUND TRIP ITSELF WENT: the typed xref map is computed in the RPC that produced the instructions and rides back on its reply, so the browser posts `buildTypedXrefMap` ZERO times on an ordinary load.** `peek-a-bin-v3uh.3` above made that upload smaller by sending `XrefInsn`s; this deletes it. `useDisassemblyRows` posts the second call over **exactly the array `hybridDisassemble` has just returned** — its xref effect is fed by the disassembly effect's result — so a whole `.text` worth of objects went back up to the worker to derive something the worker could have derived while it still held them. `hybridDisassemble`'s dispatch arm now returns `{ instructions, xrefs }` when the request sets `withXrefs`, and the client **pre-seeds `this.xrefCache`** under the identity of the array it is about to hand its caller. **No call site changed**: the hook's `buildTypedXrefMap(instructions, bounds)` resolves out of cache. Eight things (`peek-a-bin-w96b`):
  - **THE FIGURE — THREE STAMPED READINGS, AND THE SPREAD IS ITSELF THE RESULT.** `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts` already clones the same array with `bytes` (row A) and with them stripped (row D), and **D is what this deletes**. On t64.exe (60 KiB `.text`, 16844 instructions), same binary and same instructions every time: **`0870e14`** A 115.2 / D 85.1 / A−D 30.1 ms (D/A 74%, the reading the bead was decided on); **`76608fe`** A 93.6 / D 91.2 / A−D 2.4 (97%, machine loaded); **`76608fe` again, idle** A 92.3 / D 45.2 / A−D 47.0 (49%). **A is stable to ~20% and the A/D SPLIT is not stable at all** — D/A ranges 49–97% and A−D ranges 2.4–47.0 ms — so the bead's framing (“D is ~2.8× what 3a saved”) and its inverse are **both unsupportable from this harness**, and the two-reading version of this bullet, written before the third run, claimed D was the larger share “in both” and was refuted by the next re-run. **What survives all three, and is all this change needed:** D is *tens of milliseconds on a 60 KiB section*, a large fraction of A, and **linear in the section** — well over a second on a multi-megabyte `.text`, on every load. It is also **deleted outright rather than traded against anything**, so no comparison with 3a's saving has to hold for the change to be worth making.
  - **WHAT IT DOES NOT DELETE, so the arithmetic stays honest.** The entries themselves. They crossed before as `buildTypedXrefMap`'s reply and cross now as part of `hybridDisassemble`'s, so the payload *added* here is zero-sum and the saving is exactly the eliminated upload. This is not a scheme that trades one cost for a smaller one; it removes a message.
  - **THIS IS NEITHER `peek-a-bin-9a8`'s NOR `peek-a-bin-7mf`'s REFUSED CHANGE, and a future agent reading only the diff will assume it is one of them.** `9a8` refused uploading the section once under a handle, on the rule that **the key comparison must be cheaper than the work it saves** and that no key here is both cheap and sound — a content key costs the linear pass being saved, an identity key rests on an invariant nothing enforces, so a stale hit cannot be made impossible. **This has NO KEY AT ALL.** The map is derived from the array inside the very call that produced it, on the worker, so there is no content hash, no identity tuple, and nothing that can go stale; the client's own cache is keyed on an object identity it has just minted and is about to hand out. `7mf` refused **reply packing** — packing instruction BYTES into one shared buffer, whose objection is that the receiver must re-slice (row C+, which is why C is not the row to read). Nothing is packed here and the receiver unpacks nothing: `new Map(entries)` is the same line `buildTypedXrefMap`'s own reply already fed.
  - **THE ASSERTION THAT MATTERS IS THE HIT, NOT THAT TWO MAPS ARE EQUAL — because a missed seed fails SILENTLY and expensively.** The cache is keyed on the array identity **plus a bounds string**, so a seed written under a key the view never presents does not raise anything: the upload simply happens anyway, now on top of a fused payload riding along, and the change is strictly worse than not making it. `posts NO buildTypedXrefMap at all` checks `worker.posted` **before** the await — a miss posts, nothing answers it, and the promise would hang, so awaiting first reports a timeout rather than a miss. `xrefBoundsKey` is the **one declaration** of that key half for the same reason: two spellings would not fail loudly.
  - **THE FLAG IS OPT-IN, AND THAT IS WHAT KEEPS THE BLAST RADIUS AT THE CLIENT.** Only `disasmClient` sets `withXrefs`, so `src/mcp/` and the six harnesses under `corpus/` that time this arm keep receiving — and keep **measuring** — the bare `Instruction[]` they always did. That last part is load-bearing: `corpus/replyCloneCost.ts` drives this very arm to get the array it clones, and fusing unconditionally would silently change what the standing instrument measures. An absent `xrefs` means "not asked for", which is a different fact from an empty map. The control for it (fuse unconditionally) reddens two rows, one of them a pre-existing sweep-eviction test.
  - **THE MAP IS ARCH-BLIND ON PURPOSE.** `buildTypedXrefMap`'s own arm has no architecture branch — the browser calls it for an ARM64 image too — so computing the same function in the hybrid arm for **both** decoders is what makes the fused answer identical to the round trip's. `buildAllXrefs` is the arm that does split by architecture, and it is untouched.
  - **EIGHT CONTROLS, AND ONE CAME BACK INERT AND WAS FIXED BY SHARPENING THE FIXTURE RATHER THAN TUNED AWAY.** Discriminating: seed under the wrong bounds key; no seed at all; fused map empty; fuse unconditionally; client sends no bounds; **hook stops threading `imageBounds`**; hook threads the *wrong* bounds (`baseAddr` for `imageBase`, a plausible drift). **INERT on first run: computing the fused map unbounded.** The fixture's only fallback-scan operand was *inside* the image, so bounded and unbounded agreed and the row said nothing about whether the request's `imageBounds` were used. A fourth instruction shape was added — an absolute operand **outside** the image, which the bounded scan drops — and the control then reddens three rows. The two hook controls are the only ones that can see the two call sites agreeing, and they live in `DisassemblyPanel.dom.test.tsx`, not in the client's own suite.
  - **`npm run corpus` is byte-identical BY CONSTRUCTION, which is weaker than it looks and is stated deliberately.** That run goes through `FileSession`, which calls `buildTypedXrefMap` **directly** and never through the worker, so no corpus gate touches this path and a green run is evidence of confinement, not of safety. **275/275 functions byte-identical, no regression**, against a base run taken at **`76608fe`** — this branch's own base, not the session's `base-0870e14`, which is three commits behind it and carries an emitter change (`038a605`, `__try` from the `.pdata` scope table) that makes 46 functions differ for reasons that are not this change. The evidence for safety is the equivalence differential and the hit assertion, both driving the real client answered by the real `dispatch`.

  **NOT VERIFIED, and it is `9gc9`'s, `qmlz`'s and `v3uh.3`'s gap unchanged**: nothing here spawns a real `Worker`, so `structuredClone` in one process stands in for a `postMessage` across two, and every figure is an upper bound. Nothing has watched the fused reply cross a real thread boundary, and no human has seen the load in a browser (`peek-a-bin-v2u`).

- **One x86 load swept `.text` THREE TIMES, and two of those loops were the same loop.** `detectFunctions` and `buildAllXrefs` each contained a linear sweep of the whole code section — copy-paste identical down to the offset arithmetic — and App.tsx posts a *second* `buildAllXrefs` when string extraction lands after detection, so a load paid for three identical decodes. `src/disasm/linearSweep.ts` is now the one declaration (`sweepX86`) plus a session memo of it (`X86SweepCache`, in `WorkerState.x86Sweep`), which is `peek-a-bin-kis`' `Arm64SweepCache` on the other architecture. Measured at `6f2ce28`, both sides pinned: `buildAllXrefs` **777 → 59 ms** on a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image and **77 → 13** on t32, a four-RPC load **3130 → 1666 ms** (t32 409 → 243, t64 307 → 165, w32 336 → 228, w64 255 → 148), i.e. **32–47% off the whole load**. Eight things (`peek-a-bin-x40u`):
  - **IT IS NOT A TRANSCRIPTION OF `kis`, and that is the design.** A64 is fixed-width, so there the sweep *is* the disassembly and all three RPCs want the same *array*. Here only two of the three do: `hybridDisassemble` is recursive descent over a BFS work queue plus a gap fill, decoding one instruction at a time at addresses a *caller* named, and the linear sweep's grid need not have an instruction at any of them. x40u deferred it and did **not** take the number that decides it; `peek-a-bin-iqzu` did, and it shares the sweep one level down — see the entry below. `disassemble` is still out, for the reason it is out on ARM64 (it may be handed a sub-range, and the memo holds one section).
  - **The two loops were proved identical before they were merged**, not assumed: driven over t32/t64/w32/w64 and the `go` image, the two sweeps produce the same instruction stream element for element (186281 on `go`, 18280 on t32, first difference nowhere). And the merge is differentially checked — base vs change, five images, `DetectResult` and all four xref maps, plus a second `buildAllXrefs` with a different string set: **identical on all 25 comparisons**.
  - **The key is the section's bytes, and now there is ONE declaration of that rule** — `SectionMemo` in `src/disasm/sectionMemo.ts`, which `Arm64SweepCache` was moved onto as well (it had its own private `sameBytes`). It has **three** parts: the bytes byte-for-byte, the load address, and **the decoder handle by identity**, the last being a part ARM64 does not need — x86-32 and x86-64 disagree about what a byte string means and the handle is the only thing that says which reading was asked for. `transfer.ts`' test is met with room: the compare is **0.918 ms** on a 669 KiB section (1.4 ms/MiB, a JS byte loop rather than a memcpy) and it skips **754 ms** of Capstone, a margin of **820x**. Contrast `peek-a-bin-9a8`, refused because the work it proposed to save *was* a linear pass over the bytes, leaving no room under any key.
  - **`buildAllXrefs` is 94% decode and 6% resolve**, directly measured (`go`: 802 ms whole call, 754 sweep, 67 resolve). So the split the bead asked about is real and it is what the memo exposes: on a hit the resolve re-runs in full over the new string set, which is the whole point of the second call, and the sweep does not. A memo that also cached the *result* would answer the first build's question twice — pinned by a test.
  - **Retention is the price and it was measured against what is already held.** The sweep is **~135 B/instruction — 24 MB for the `go` image, 2.3 MB for t32, 37–43x the section** — for as long as the session holds the image. The app *already* retains `hybridDisassemble`'s `Instruction[]` for the same section in `AppState`: **55.2 MB and 6.3 MB, at 372 B/insn**. So this adds 44% to a term already dominated by an array 2.7x heavier per element, and it is lighter precisely because `SweptInsn` is four fields and no `bytes` view — neither consumer reads one. **Interning was measured and refused**: pooling mnemonics takes 24.1 → 19.8 MB and pooling `opStr` too takes it to 17.7 (165 and 40616 distinct), sweep unchanged at 735/716/739 ms — 27% of a term that is 44% of the dominant one, bought with a second mechanism whose 40k-entry pool has retention of its own.
  - **THE VERIFICATION GAP THE BEAD NAMED IS CLOSED, and it was real: no harness drives the worker RPC path at all** — `npm run corpus` goes through `FileSession`, which has no worker and no session state. `disasmClient.test.ts` now posts a whole load through the real client and answers it with the real `dispatch`, and is **negative-controlled in four directions**: a memo that never stores (4 tests fail), a memo keyed on length-and-address only (3 fail here plus **2 on the ARM64 side**, which is the proof `Arm64SweepCache` really routes through the shared rule now), the decoder dropped from the key (2 fail), and a stale-serving memo asserted to produce the *wrong* call graph so that the content-key test is known to discriminate.
  - **`npm run corpus` is the control and is byte-identical**, because the MCP path passes no memo: 1475 artifact files, `diff -r` reporting only the label line, `compare.mjs` "no regression" with `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0` and emitted C byte-identical throughout. `npm run corpus:arm64` **43/43 green and its output byte-identical to base**, with the gated sweep-cache differential still re-deriving 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131. `npm run corpus:comments` byte-identical, all four x86 md5s unmoved.
  - **RESIDUE — a dead heuristic rather than a defect, and it has since been DELETED rather than revived (`peek-a-bin-7lue`; see the gotcha below).** The one thing the old loop knew that a flat array does not is where a *gap* was — it reset `prevWasUnconditional` on an empty decode — and x40u preserved that (`insn.address !== prevEnd`) so the refactor was behaviour-preserving by construction. But `prevWasUnconditional`'s only reader was provably inert, and 7lue then measured that reviving it would be *worse* than inert. The sweep's own side of the contract — that a gap is visible at all — is still pinned in `linearSweep.test.ts`, and its live consumer is now `gridScan` (`peek-a-bin-iqzu`), which stops serving a run where the grid stops being contiguous.
- **`hybridDisassemble` WAS THE LAST x86 RPC DECODING `.text` FOR ITSELF, AND THE COINCIDENCE RATE IS ~100%.** `peek-a-bin-x40u` left it unwired and left the deciding number untaken, with a comment in `dispatch.ts` pointing at itself "for the coincidence rate" — a dangling promise, now fixed. It was the largest remaining RPC: **765 ms of a 1666 ms `go` load** and 125/70/123/65 ms on t32/t64/w32/w64. It cannot take the sweep's *array* — recursive descent over a BFS work queue plus a gap fill produces a different, annotated, smaller stream, one instruction at a time at addresses a *caller* named — so what it takes is the **decoder underneath**: `gridScan` (`linearSweep.ts`) is a `CapstoneScan` answering from the held grid where the grid has an instruction at that address and delegating to Capstone where it does not, and all three phases keep their loops verbatim. Measured at `1198f4a`, both sides pinned in one process: hybrid **770 → 203 ms** on the `go` image, 133 → 22 (t32), 71 → 20 (t64), 115 → 14 (w32), 62 → 10 (w64) — and whole loads **1654 → 1018, 241 → 128, 164 → 96, 206 → 117, 137 → 82**, a further 38–47% on top of x40u's 32–47%. Nine things (`peek-a-bin-iqzu`):
  - **THE RATE IS 99.9–100.0% OVER 222137 INSTRUCTIONS, and 100.0% of the hits agree** in mnemonic, operands and size. Per phase: 100.0% `.pdata`, 100.0% BFS, 99.0–100.0% gap fill. The whole residue is 26 instructions on t32 and 22 on w32, and **0 on all three x64 images**. A prediction of 96–97% was recorded first and was too pessimistic, and the gap is a bug in the prediction: a sweep walking into data comes out misaligned, which *adds* spurious grid entries rather than removing real ones, so the grid is a **superset** almost everywhere — 186281 entries against the 155531 this method wants on `go`, 18280 against 18045 on t32.
  - **A RATE IS NOT A CORRECTNESS ARGUMENT and the differential is.** The addresses lining up says nothing about the instructions. Served against decoded through the real `dispatch` with real Capstone, the returned `Instruction[]` is identical **element for element and field for field — `bytes`, `source` and `comment` included — 0 differing on all five images**.
  - **PEEK, never `sweep`.** The memo holds one slot, so a `get` here would both pay for a sweep this method does not need and **evict the section** the other two RPCs share. Peeking makes it strictly additive: a miss leaves the method decoding exactly as before and leaves the slot exactly as it found it. It costs nothing because the ordering is *structural*, not lucky — `useDisassemblyRows.ts` posts this only when `state.functions` is non-empty, i.e. after `detectFunctions` filled the slot. **The live miss is a hex patch**, which hands this method a modified copy of the section; the content key declines it and the fall-back is the whole of the fix. Negative-controlled: `sweep` in place of `peek` fails the eviction test.
  - **A SERVED `bytes` MUST BE A PRIVATE `.slice()`, never a `subarray` of the section.** It reaches the view as `Instruction.bytes` (the hex column, read in six components) and therefore crosses `postMessage` in the reply, where a view would make the structured clone serialise **the whole `.text` once per instruction**. Every field reads identically either way, so nothing else in the repo would catch it; pinned by a test and by the harness's `own bytes buffers` row. Note the property is *not aliasing the section*, **not** "the buffer is exactly `size` long" — capstone-wasm backs each of its own records with a fixed 24-byte `HEAPU8.slice`, so an exact test calls all 18045 of t32's decoded instructions defects, which is what a first draft of that row did report.
  - **Three rules, each a way to be wrong, each negative-controlled.** A miss delegates (the grid may lack an address either because the sweep stepped over it or because the decoder refused it, and decoding is right for both). A run stops where the grid stops being **contiguous** — `cs_disasm` returns instructions until a byte it cannot decode, and a discontinuity is that byte recorded. And the caller's **window** still bounds the run (`CS_WINDOW_BYTES`, `limit`, the buffer), or the caller's `offset` advance differs from what it advanced by before.
  - **A binary search, not a map — no third structure over the same bytes.** `sweepX86` emits strictly ascending addresses, so 18 comparisons cost **0.076 µs** against the **3.7 µs** decode they replace. Measured beside it: building a `Map<number, SweptInsn>` is 0.13 µs/insn and tens of MB on a large image. Serving costs **0.165–0.251 µs/insn**, so serve + lookup is **6.8–8.6% of a batch decode and 3.9–5.4% of a `decodeOne`** — an 11–25x margin on `transfer.ts`' rule, and the key compare itself is *already paid for* by the other two RPCs, which is the argument FOR and is why the marginal cost here is a lookup rather than a byte compare.
  - **Unsorted input degrades safely.** A binary search that misses returns −1 and delegates, and the contiguity check breaks early — so a violation of the sweep's ordering costs performance and never correctness.
  - **THE x40u TEST STUB WAS NOT A FUNCTION OF THE BYTES, and this is what exposed it.** `disasmClient.test.ts`' `countingX86` chose its mnemonic and operands by `i === 4` — an offset *within whatever window it was handed* — so it answered differently for a whole-section sweep than for the 15-byte per-address windows the BFS uses, and the serve and the decode it replaces provably disagreed *about the stub*. Fixed to key on the absolute address, which is the property every real decoder has and the one this whole scheme rests on. Do not put an `i`-relative rule back.
  - **The instrument is landed**: `npm run corpus:gridserve -- <path>` (`corpus/hybridGridServe.ts`), reporting the rate per phase, the differential and the timing, driving the real `dispatch` with a **wrapped** Capstone handle (`corpus/arm64.ts`' rule — an instrument belongs outside the code it judges), so nothing replicates the three loops. Negative-controlled three ways: a subarray serve turns `own bytes buffers` NO; a wrong mnemonic takes `differing` to 18046/16845; a grid shifted one byte takes the rate to 18.8%/5.3%, which is what makes the 100% a measurement rather than a tautology. **`npm run corpus` is the control and is byte-identical** (`diff -r` finds only the two label lines; `compare.mjs` "no regression", `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0`, emitted C byte-identical throughout), `npm run corpus:arm64` output **byte-identical to base** with the gated differential still 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131, and `npm run corpus:comments` byte-identical with all four x86 md5s unmoved.
- **…AND THE LOAD WAS POSTING A FIFTH, UN-MEMOIZED WHOLE-SECTION DECODE THAT IT THREW AWAY.** `useDisassemblyRows`' disassembly effect chooses `hybridDisassemble` when `state.functions` is non-empty and a plain whole-section `disassemble` otherwise. On a load `state.functions` is `[]` — `RESET` empties it — `activeTab` defaults to `"disassembly"`, and `App.tsx` mounts that tab in the same commit, so the effect fires **before detection has answered**, takes the `else` arm, and posts the fallback over the whole of `.text`. Detection lands, `SET_FUNCTIONS` re-runs the effect, `hybridDisassemble` is posted, and the first answer is discarded unread. Since `peek-a-bin-v3uh.2` that arm is gated on `!ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS[state.analysisPhase]`, so it fires only where "no functions" is a settled answer and never during a load. Eight things:
  - **THE THROWAWAY ARM IS THE ONE x86 DECODE PATH WITH NO MEMO, and that is deliberate rather than an oversight to fix on the worker side.** `dispatch.ts` routes `disassemble` **around** `WorkerState.x86Sweep` — and `disassembleArm64` around `arm64Sweep` — with the reason written at both sites: the method may be handed a sub-range, the memo holds exactly one section, so a sub-range decode taking the slot would cost the next `buildAllXrefs` a whole sweep. `disasmClient.test.ts`' `does not let a single-function decode evict the section` gates that. So `peek-a-bin-x40u` and `peek-a-bin-iqzu` cut three of the load's four decodes to one shared sweep and this fifth one was, by construction, exempt from the saving. **Wiring it into the memo would have been the wrong fix**: it would re-open the eviction hole for a request that should not be sent at all.
  - **AND THE WORKER IS SERIAL, so the discarded request was very likely serviced FIRST.** `App.tsx` posts `configure().then(detectFunctions)` — one promise hop — while the view's effect posts from a *lazily-loaded child*, and a child's effect runs before its parent's. That is the same effect-ordering fact `peek-a-bin-x4o2` is written about; it was used there to argue about WHICH DECODER the request got and was never costed as duplicated work. Serviced first, the throwaway pass delays detection by its own whole duration, and detection is the load's budget-setter (`REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`).
  - **THE COST IS FOUR THINGS, not one, and the retention is the one that outlives the load.** Per load, on top of the sweep the memo already saves: one whole-section Capstone linear pass; one whole `Instruction[]` reply clone; one extra `buildTypedXrefMap` round trip, because the throwaway instructions reach `useDisassemblyRows`' xref effect and it posts for them too; and **permanent retention of a second whole-section instruction array** in the client's `disasmCache` under the non-`"hybrid:"` key, held for the session. `peek-a-bin-x40u` measured a held sweep at **~135 B/instruction — 24 MB for the 669 KiB-`.text` `go` image**; this is a second array of the same order, of instructions nothing will ever render.
  - **NO NEW TIMING WAS TAKEN AND THE CASE IS STRUCTURAL — say so rather than quoting a number that is not this one.** The nearest measured figures are quotations from the two beads above: an un-memoized whole-section sweep through `buildAllXrefs` was **729 ms on a 669 KiB `.text`** at `6f2ce28` (`peek-a-bin-x40u`), and `hybridDisassemble` decoding for itself was **798 ms on the same image** at `peek-a-bin-iqzu`. The discarded pass is that shape of work. It is *not* a measurement of this change, and nothing here spawns a real `Worker` — the same gap `9gc9`, `qmlz` and `x40u` all record.
  - **THE GATE IS `ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS`, NEVER A HAND-WRITTEN PHASE CHAIN, and the four legitimately-no-functions cases are why it has to be the record.** `"ready"` with an empty list (a PE32 image detecting nothing, or a run with omitted passes), `"failed"` (including the `state.disasmFailed` arm), `"no-code"` and `"timed-out"` are all terminal and all `false` in that `Record<AnalysisPhase, boolean>`, so every one still reaches the fallback and still gets a linear listing. A hand-written `phase === "ready"` would withhold the listing from three of them, which is `peek-a-bin-bo3b` and `peek-a-bin-b3jn` exactly — and is the second negative control below. The boolean is extracted **above** the effect and listed in its dependency array, on the model of the `imageBase`/`sizeOfImage` numbers above the xref effect: it flips at most twice per load, where `state.analysisPhase` itself would re-run the whole disassembly effect on every phase transition.
  - **`disassembling` IS LEFT TRUE ACROSS THE EARLY RETURN**, so the pane keeps its `Disassembling...` spinner rather than falling through to an empty listing that is about to be replaced. Asserted directly, because "posted nothing" and "showed nothing" would otherwise be one row. It also covers the *second* load of a session, where `DisassemblyView` stays mounted and the hook's `instructions` state still holds the previous file's stream until `hybridDisassemble` answers: that window is not new in kind — the discarded request was asynchronous too, so the stale array outlived the old effect's first line in exactly the same way — only longer, and the spinner is what stood in front of it both times. Clearing the array on the early return was refused: it would make the xref effect post a `new Map()` for a state nothing renders.
  - **Two negative controls, both discriminating, neither inert.** (1) Drop the `analysisSettled` arm so the fallback always fires: the three in-flight rows redden and the two settled rows stay green — the prediction, exactly. (2) Spell the gate `state.analysisPhase === "ready"` instead of reading the record: the table-driven settled row reddens and nothing else does. The settled and in-flight rows are both **driven over `ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS` itself** rather than over a list of phase names, so a phase added later that joins on the wrong side fails here as well as at the build — the shape `Sidebar.dom.test.tsx` and `FileLoader.dom.test.tsx` already use.
  - **IT MAKES TWO WRITTEN-DOWN PREMISES TRUE, and both were false.** (a) `corpus/rpcUploadCost.ts`' `SENDS_PER_LOAD = 4` is read off the call sites rather than measured, and it was an **under-count**: the fallback made it 5 on every load. `checkOneRegion` re-derives the *premise* behind the constant — that the sends are all of one region — but nothing re-derived the COUNT, so the harness has been over-stating an upload-once scheme's saving by a fifth. It is right now, and the module's commentary says which commit made it so. (b) `disasmClient.test.ts`' `hybridDisassemble decodes through the held sweep` describes its `load()` helper with "`useDisassemblyRows.ts` posts `hybridDisassemble` only when `state.functions` is non-empty, i.e. only after `detectFunctions` has answered — which is what fills the slot". That was false when written: the view also posted the ungated `disassemble`, and the slot the describe block is about was filled by a request nobody kept. The sentence is **kept and annotated** rather than deleted, since it is now a true statement of the invariant the helper rests on. **Both `load()` helpers additionally gained the `buildTypedXrefMap` the view posts once the instructions land** — they modelled a load with no xref map in it at all — and it is posted from inside the dispatch sequence, not up front, because it can only follow the disassembly reply. It ships the instruction list rather than the section and decodes nothing, so it moves neither `sweeps` nor `hybridDecodes`; the `iqzu` equivalence test now compares the resulting map as well as the stream, so the served-equals-decoded claim is asserted one step past the method under test.
- **`FileLoader`'s FOUR-STEP PROGRESS PANEL COULD NEVER RENDER, AND THE FOURTH HAND-WRITTEN PHASE CHAIN WENT WITH IT — DELETED, NOT RELOCATED.** The component computed `isAnalyzing = analysisPhase !== "idle" && analysisPhase !== "ready"` — the shape `ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS` exists to replace, and **one term shorter than the three sites `peek-a-bin-bo3b` converted**, missing `"failed"` as well as `"no-code"` and `"timed-out"` — and gated three things on it: a four-step panel keyed off an `ANALYSIS_STEPS` table, `disabled` plus four `undefined` handlers on the drop zone, and suppression of the recents list. **None of it was reachable, and the reason lives in another file.** `App.tsx` renders `FileLoader` only when `!state.peFile`, and `handleFile` dispatches RESET, SET_LOADING, SET_ANALYSIS_PHASE `"parsing"` and SET_PE_FILE from **one synchronous callback** with no `await` between them; React cannot commit a render mid-callback (and `main.tsx` uses `createRoot`, so there is no legacy unbatched mode either), so by the first paint after a drop either `peFile` is set — and the component is unmounted — or `parsePE` threw and the catch dispatched `"idle"`, **not** a terminal phase. That single line is what kept the common case correct. `loading` could not rescue the panel either: it is the ONLY prop that gated the panel independently, and both SET_PE_FILE and SET_ERROR clear it inside the same batch.
  - **THE UNREACHABILITY IS MEASURED, NOT REASONED, AND THE PROBE HAD A DISCRIMINATING CONTROL.** A throwaway probe replaced `FileLoader` with a recorder reading `useAppState()` from its own mount point and drove a real `buildMinimalPE64()` load through the real `App`: **exactly two commits while mounted, both `{loading: false, phase: "idle"}`**. Inserting a single `await Promise.resolve()` **after** SET_LOADING turned it red with `{loading: true, phase: "parsing"}` — so the probe can see the panel becoming reachable, and what would make the deleted code live again is precisely any yield between SET_LOADING and SET_PE_FILE. A first attempt at that control placed the yield *above* SET_LOADING and came back **INERT**, which is recorded rather than tuned away: it does not split the batch that matters. The probe was a measurement, not a deliverable, and was **deleted** — nothing depends on the premise any more, so a standing guard there would fail for a benign future change.
  - **DELETION BEAT RELOCATION ON THE HOUSE'S OWN TERMS.** `ANALYSIS_STEPS` was **a second phase→label table grouping the same union differently** from `StatusBar.phaseLabels`, so moving the panel to a surface that can render would have meant keeping two tables for one fact — the class this repo closes. The steps a user actually waits on (detect functions, build xrefs) all run *after* this screen is gone; what they see is the sidebar skeleton and the status-bar spinner, **both already reading `ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS`**. The conservative fallback — convert `isAnalyzing` to `ANALYSIS_IN_PROGRESS[analysisPhase]` and leave the rest — is strictly weaker: it leaves the dead panel and the second table standing.
  - **THE PROPS COLLAPSED FROM FIVE TO TWO, and `fileName` was the surprise.** `analysisPhase` fed only `isAnalyzing` and `getStepStatus`; **`fileName`'s only use in the whole component was the panel's "Analyzing …" line**, so it died with it; `loading`'s other two uses were inert by the same argument (`disabled={loading || loadingExample}` became `disabled={loadingExample}`, the local flag being the one a render can observe, and the recents guard dropped both suppressing terms). What is left is `onFile` and `error`. **`getStepStatus`'s `"failed"` arm was separately redundant even inside the dead code** — no `ANALYSIS_STEPS` entry lists `"failed"`, so `activeStepIndex` is −1 and the next line returns `"pending"` anyway; that had already been found by a negative control and written into the suite.
  - **THE TRIPWIRE WENT WITH ITS SUBJECT, AS ITS OWN INSTRUCTIONS SAID.** `FileLoader.dom.test.tsx` carried a block headed "A LATENT HAZARD, PINNED AS A TRIPWIRE" plus two tests characterising the divergence, written to go red on exactly this change and saying so: *"Converting this component … would make both of them fail — that is the intended notification and NOT a regression. If you make that change, delete this whole block."* In the event the tripwire fired **at compile time rather than at runtime** — removing the props reddened ten `tsc` errors in that file — which is the stronger notification. Nine tests were deleted with their subject (the six-test progress `describe`, the two tripwire tests, and "hides the list while an analysis is running", whose `!isAnalyzing` guard no longer exists): `FileLoader.dom.test.tsx` **19 → 10**, `--dir src` **4989 → 4980** at `c3f3ba2`, no change in file count. The `peek-a-bin-1xc5` precedent — count them as deleted-with-their-subject, do not renumber the history.
  - **THE CHANGE IS INERT IN THE APP AND THAT MUST BE SAID PLAINLY.** Every phase the two predicates disagree about is unreachable while the component is mounted, so the fallback conversion would have moved nothing at runtime and the deletion moves nothing either — it removes only code that could not execute. The discriminating evidence is the probe above and the component-level tripwire, never app behaviour; `App.dom.test.tsx` drives real loads and is **unchanged and green**, which is corroboration that nothing observable moved, not proof the code was dead.
  - **CONSEQUENCE LEFT STANDING ON PURPOSE: `AppState.loading` now has NO READER.** `state.loading` had exactly one consumer in the tree, the prop this change removed, so `SET_LOADING` is write-only state. It is deliberately **not** swept up here — deleting a reducer action touches the `AppAction` union and `appReducer.test.ts`'s branch-by-branch coverage, which is a reducer bead rather than a component one. **Cancellation and an elapsed-time readout are also explicitly deferred**: the drop zone's old `disabled` state offered neither, there is no abort channel, the disasm worker services serially, and real cancellation means `terminate()` plus a rebuild discarding `X86SweepCache`, `Arm64SweepCache`, `CallSummaryCache`, `StructRegistry` and the Capstone init. (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.13`)

- **`detectFunctions` IS 86-89% ONE SHARED CAPSTONE SWEEP, and the "13x the next RPC" reading of that is arithmetic rather than a diagnosis.** After `peek-a-bin-x40u` and `peek-a-bin-iqzu` cut `buildAllXrefs` and `hybridDisassemble` roughly threefold each, detection was left as the whole load-time budget and the sole constraint on `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`. It had never been broken down; `npm run corpus:detectcost -- <path>` (`corpus/detectPhaseCost.ts`) is the per-phase instrument, fed by an optional `phaseTap` on `detectFunctions` itself. Measured at `e9e6eaa`+HEAD over nine images — the four corpus binaries and five Windows PEs from `go` (both `GOARCH`es, 48 KiB to 2359 KiB of `.text`), medians, ms (peek-a-bin-6dv3):

  | phase | share of detection, x86-64 (5 images) | share, x86-32 (4 images) |
  |---|---|---|
  | `sweep` — the shared linear sweep | **85.6-89.0%** | **73.6-81.0%** |
  | `interior-starts` | 0.0-0.1% | 8.0-10.3% |
  | `tail-calls` | 4.8-8.6% | 4.6-8.1% |
  | `sweep-scan` — detection's own pass over the swept array | 3.4-4.6% | 2.4-3.7% |
  | `seh32-relation` | 0.2-0.3% | 1.4-4.3% |
  | `prologue-scan` | 1.3-1.6% | 0.4-0.6% |
  | `padding-scan` | 0.2% | 0.1-0.2% |
  | `pdata-seeds`, `handler-seeds`, `entry-point`, `exports`, `pattern-admit`, `sizes`, `thunk-names` | ≤0.1% each | ≤0.1% each |

  Six things, and the first is the one to carry:
  - **THE COMPARISON THE BEAD MAKES IS UNFAIR IN A SPECIFIC WAY, and reversing it is the finding.** Detection is the load's *first* RPC, so it is the one that fills `X86SweepCache`; the other two are cheap **because it already paid**. Detection's own work — everything but `sweep` — is 9/10/81/112/**270** ms on the x64 images against `hybridDisassemble`'s 27/22/176/198/**732** and `buildAllXrefs`' 8/18/69/81/**192**, i.e. on every image measured it is *smaller than either successor*. Read the same quantity end to end rather than by subtraction: detection with the memo already holding the section is **9/9/83/116/273 ms** (the harness's `warm` column), against 64-2354 cold, and the two readings of the sweep agree to 0.7-3.8%. So there is no 13x; there is one section decode, and whichever RPC ran first would pay it.
  - **NOTHING IS SUPERLINEAR, and the fit is over a 37-38x spread in both architectures.** Per swept instruction the whole of detection is **3.90-4.15 µs on x64 and 4.41-4.59 µs on PE32, spread 1.1x and 1.0x** across 15504→581748 and 16814→646274 instructions and 275→5314 and 265→5101 functions. Every phase's own per-instruction rate is flat to within 2x, the exceptions being sub-microsecond rows that are noise. In particular `interiorBranchedOverStarts` — the O(starts × jumps) shape a reader would suspect — is **0.353-0.472 µs/insn, spread 1.3x**: its binary-search prefilter works at scale. So the ~1100-1700 ms/MiB is a *rate*, and 300 s is reached at roughly 175-280 MiB of code depending on the image, which is `peek-a-bin-8c6v`'s figure re-derived and not a shape problem to be fixed here.
  - **AND THE RATE IS CAPSTONE'S, NOT OURS.** A throwaway probe stepping the identical windows with a bare `cs.disasm` and building nothing on top reads **3.38-3.66 µs/insn against `sweepX86`'s 3.79-4.14** and returns the identical instruction counts, so **82-93% of the sweep is inside capstone-wasm** and 7-18% is `createScan`'s windowing plus building `SweptInsn`. Combining the two measurements, **~76-79% of x64 `detectFunctions` is inside capstone-wasm's decode of the section**. Detail is off (nothing here calls `setOption`, and Capstone's default is off), so what is left is the decoder plus capstone-wasm's per-instruction marshalling out of WASM memory. That is the whole remaining lever on this tool's budget and it is **not in `functionDetect.ts`** — filed as `peek-a-bin-62zy`, **and `peek-a-bin-fdi8` has since TAKEN it**: the split that bead asked for showed the marshalling was the larger half by 5 to 1, and `disasm/capstoneReader.ts` replaced it. **The figures in this bullet are therefore the pre-`fdi8` ones and must not be quoted as current.** Re-measured at `adfe1b5`, the sweep's share of `detectFunctions` falls from the 85.6-89.0% (x64) / 73.6-81.0% (PE32) tabulated below to **66.5-76.9%** (t32 66.5%, w64 74.4%, a 669 KiB-`.text` `go` build 76.9%) — not because any other phase grew, but because the sweep got ~3.4x cheaper and they did not. Read the whole table below the same way.
  - **So the answer to "what should change in detection" is: almost nothing, and this is the measured refusal.** Making every non-sweep phase free would cut detection by 11-14% on x64 and 19-26% on PE32, and a load by 8-11% and 14-19%.
