The two candidates worth naming and why each was declined: `tail-calls`, the largest non-sweep phase on x64, is one Capstone call per function over a 15-byte tail, and serving it from the grid — `iqzu`'s mechanism, and the grid is right there — would decode the tail under the *sweep's* alignment instead of from `end - 15`, so it can change which instruction is judged to be last and therefore `fn.tailCallTarget`; that is not a pure-performance change and `npm run corpus` could not be expected to stay byte-identical. `sweep-scan`'s `recentInsns` window costs 186k object copies and shifts on the `go` image and could be a `slice` of the swept array taken only at an indirect `jmp`, but it feeds `readAbsoluteTable`/`boundedCaseCount`/`overlappedTableExtent` — `peek-a-bin-padl`, `peek-a-bin-xqxy` and `peek-a-bin-mk42`'s shared input — for 3-4%.
  - **THE ONE THING THAT WAS TAKEN is the SEH32 prologue head, and it was taken because the work was provably UNREAD rather than merely expensive.** `headInstructions` decoded `MAX_SEH32_HEAD_INSNS` (8) instructions at every candidate start before `seh32PrologImmediates` saw any of them, and that rule stops at the first instruction that is neither padding nor a pushed immediate — which is nearly every function's second. Instrumented over t32/w32 and two Windows/x86 `go` builds: **8.00 decoded per head against 1.00-1.89 consumed**. It is a pull now (`HeadReader` in `seh32.ts`), so the instructions the rule sees are the same instructions in the same order and the ones it never asked for are simply not decoded — output-neutral by construction rather than by measurement, which is the property that matters because this feeds `interiorBranchedOverStarts`' fifth admission and therefore function boundaries. `seh32-relation` **15.30/14.17/97.29/262.30 → 3.29/3.51/16.33/39.94 ms** (w32/t32/x86/x86big, −76% to −85%), i.e. 8.3-17.1% of PE32 detection → 1.4-4.3%; **x64 is untouched** because `is64` takes the empty-map arm and `headReader` is never called. `npm run corpus` **byte-identical** (`diff -r` finds only the label line and the artifact path; `compare.mjs` "no regression", `CHANGED 0 / only-base 0 / only-change 0`, emitted C byte-identical on all four binaries), `corpus:arm64` 51/51 with the differential still 4083 → 1361 and 3393 → 1131. Negative-controlled: restoring the eager fill fails exactly one test.
  - **The instrument checks its own boundaries, and one of its three controls is INERT — read that before trusting a column.** Deleting a `phase()` call takes `unattributed` from 0.2% to 10.7% and fails the ordering test (it caught a real omission: `phase("pdata-seeds")` was never inserted and its cost was being reported as `handler-seeds`). Reporting `sweep` *before* the sweep runs leaves `unattributed` at -0.0% and makes the cold-minus-warm cross-check disagree by six orders of magnitude. But moving the `sweep`/`sweep-scan` boundary past the scan loop fools **both**: no boundary is missing and `sweep-scan` is 4% of `sweep` against a ~10% run-to-run spread. That boundary rests on reading the code. The harness also replicates `dispatch`'s argument construction and prints `same answer as dispatch` for it, because a drifted replica would report a confident split of a call the worker never makes.
- **ANNOTATIONS WERE KEYED ON THE FILE NAME AND NOTHING ELSE, WHICH MADE THIS THE ONE PLACE IN THE APP WHERE A USER SILENTLY LOST WORK.** (Summarised in `CLAUDE.md` under **Conventions → localStorage** rather than in its gotchas list, because the rule it states is where the store's keys come from.) `App.tsx` read and wrote `localStorage["peek-a-bin:" + state.fileName]`. So: drop `v1/setup.exe`, rename forty functions, drop `v2/setup.exe` — and you get **v1's renames applied to v2's addresses**, because the two builds share a key and the addresses are not the same code; then v2's first save, which happens on load because the persist effect runs immediately, **overwrites v1's record permanently**. Comparing two builds of one binary is the single most ordinary reverse-engineering workflow there is. The mirror case is quieter and just as bad: renaming the same build on disk (`setup.exe` → `setup_v1_final.exe`) moved the key and lost every annotation it had. And the third face of it is the namespace — `peek-a-bin:` is **flat**, shared with about eighteen real settings keys (`sidebar-width`, `sections-open`, `graph-overview-open`, `callers-open`, `callgraph-height`, `bottom-panel-height`, `view-mode`, `show-bytes`, `scroll-sync`, `decompile-width`, `chat-width`, `chat:${fileName}`, `theme-id`, `custom-themes`, `font-size`, `llm-profiles`, `llm-settings`, `decompile-server`, plus the orphaned `report:${fileName}`) — so a file *named* `font-size` wrote its bookmarks over a setting, and `FileLoader`'s recents scan carried a four-name deny-list against that population. `src/utils/annotationKey.ts` is now the one declaration of the key, of the migration, of the prefix scan and of the one targeted deletion. (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.5`)
  - **THE KEY: `peek-a-bin:annotations:<size>-<timeDateStamp, 8 lowercase hex>[-<CodeView PDB GUID>]`, and every field in it was chosen for being available SYNCHRONOUSLY.** `buffer.byteLength` and `coffHeader.timeDateStamp` are both on `parsePE`'s own answer; the GUID comes from `parseDebugDirectory`, which is not part of `parsePE` (it is a lazy memo in `HeaderView.tsx`) but is header-local and bounded in both directions (`MAX_DEBUG_DIRECTORY_ENTRIES` × `MAX_PDB_PATH_BYTES`), so calling it at load costs what that panel's own call costs. The key is only ever *compared*, never parsed back, which is why the GUID's own dashes are harmless.
  - **THE METRICS-WORKER CONTENT HASH WAS REFUSED, ON COST RATHER THAN ON STRENGTH.** A digest over the whole image is the obviously stronger identity and the metrics worker already exists to compute exactly that off-thread. But it makes annotation **load async**, and the two effects are the whole problem: the load effect would `await` a digest while the user can already bookmark and rename — a rename made inside that window is written under the wrong key or lost outright — and the persist effect would need its own `await` plus a cancellation token, on a path that currently fires on every keystroke-sized state change. **The harm being fixed (two same-named builds) is smaller than the harm that would be introduced (a race on every load).** `size` + `timeDateStamp` costs nothing and needs neither.
  - **THE GUID IS APPENDED, NOT SUBSTITUTED, AND THAT DIRECTION IS THE ARGUMENT.** Appending can only ever **split** two identities apart; substituting could **merge** them, and a merge is the direction that loses work — the same "refusing is the benign direction" reasoning `structs.ts` uses for a struct candidate. It costs one thing, stated rather than implied: a stripped copy of a build that still has its debug directory is a *different* key, so annotations do not follow a binary across a strip. The property is asserted directly (`withGuid.startsWith(bare)`), not described.
  - **THE HONEST RESIDUAL, and it is at the declaration as well as here.** Two builds with the same size AND the same `timeDateStamp` AND no debug directory still collide. Reaching that needs a stripped, same-size, non-content-stamped pair. Worth noting the opposite direction too, because the field's name understates it: **MSVC's `/Brepro` writes a hash of the content into `timeDateStamp`**, so on a reproducible build this key is *already* content-derived.
  - **THE MIGRATION IS MANDATORY, ONE-TIME, AND NON-DESTRUCTIVE — AND THE LEGACY KEY IS LEFT EXACTLY WHERE IT IS.** On load, when the build key holds nothing a reader should apply, a legacy `peek-a-bin:<fileName>` blob that passes `validateAnnotations` is adopted and written forward. It is **not deleted**, for two reasons and the first is the stronger: a user who reverts to an older build of the tool must still find their work, so deleting on adoption makes the migration one-way and silent; and a deleter on the **load path** has to decide which keys are annotation records, with nothing enforcing that `peek-a-bin:<name>` stays a prefix of nothing else — the exact foot-gun already refused for the orphaned `peek-a-bin:report:` keys. So legacy records are **intentionally orphaned on that precedent**, a few KB the user can clear from devtools. A useful side effect: because the legacy key survives, the migration is **idempotent**, so the transient described below cannot lose it.
  - **THE TRIGGER IS "ABSENT OR MALFORMED", NOT "ABSENT", AND THE DIFFERENCE IS DECIDED BY A REAL CASE.** The persist effect writes an empty record the instant a file opens, so the *second* load of any migrated file finds a valid-and-empty current record. Re-adopting the legacy blob there would **resurrect annotations the user had deleted**, so a current record wins whenever it validates, including when it is empty. Widening past that — falling back to legacy when the current record is present but *malformed* — is strictly better than stranding the data and is pinned in both directions.
  - **A PRE-EXISTING SAME-TICK TRANSIENT, LEFT AS IT WAS AND WORTH KNOWING.** The load effect is declared above the persist effect, so in the commit where the key first becomes non-null both run: the load dispatches `LOAD_PERSISTED` (queued, not yet applied) and the persist then writes the *pre-load* state — an empty record — before the re-render writes the real payload back. The final state is correct and the window is one tick. It is not new (the two effects were in this order before), and the non-destructive migration is what makes even the pathological case recoverable: the legacy key is still there next load.
  - **`fileName` MOVED INTO THE STORED VALUE, and that is a consequence rather than a decoration.** Once the key is not the name, the name has to come back *out*: the recents list on the loader screen is a list of file names and now finds records by scanning the prefix. `AnnotationRecord` is `AnnotationPayload` plus an optional `fileName`, which `validateAnnotations` ignores, so no existing reader had to change. A record whose `fileName` disagrees with the file currently open **is not a mismatch** — it is the same build under a new name, which is precisely the case this module exists to keep working.
  - **`KNOWN_LS_KEYS` IS DELETED AND IT WAS INERT — do not read the deletion as a bug fix.** `FileLoader.tsx` walked `peek-a-bin:` and skipped four hand-written settings names against a namespace of about eighteen, which reads as a rule that holds and does not. What actually kept `sidebar-width` out of the recents list was the annotation-count test after it (`bookmarks + renames + comments > 0`), since a settings value has none of those fields; the deny-list never rejected anything the count test would have admitted. With a prefix of its own, "is this key an annotation record" is a prefix test, so the list stops being needed rather than stops being wrong. `listAnnotationRecords` is the one declaration of that scan and is asserted against all four old names plus five more settings keys plus a legacy annotation blob.
  - **THE COST OF THE PREFIX SCAN, PAID KNOWINGLY: a LEGACY record is no longer LISTED.** The recents list on the loader screen shows an annotations-only row for a file whose cached bytes were evicted but whose annotations survive. That scan is now the `peek-a-bin:annotations:` namespace alone, so a pre-migration record produces no row until the file is opened once (which adopts it). The **data is not lost** — the migration is on the load path and the legacy key is never deleted — only the row is, and only until the next open. Making the scan read bare `peek-a-bin:<x>` keys too would mean deciding which of them are files and which are settings, which *is* the deny-list this change exists to remove. Asserted in both directions in `FileLoader.dom.test.tsx`: the legacy row is absent, and all four names the old deny-list carried plus `font-size` produce no row either.
  - **THE ONE PLACE DELETING IS STILL RIGHT is `removeAnnotationsFor`, and it is on the click path, not the load path.** "Remove this recent" deletes every build-keyed record saved under that name *and* that name's legacy key — the user asked to be rid of it. It is keyed on the NAME because the recents list still is on the other side too, and where two builds share a name both records go, which is what the row the user clicked claims to be. Another build's record and an unrelated setting are asserted untouched, so it is a targeted removal and never a prefix sweep.
  - **THE IndexedDB HALF WAS DELIBERATELY A SEPARATE BEAD AND IS NOW CLOSED — see the entry below.** `utils/recentFiles.ts` used `keyPath: "name"`, so v2 also silently **evicted v1's cached bytes**. Fixing it meant a DB version bump with an `onupgradeneeded` that rewrites every record under a composite key, and the harm it prevents is *cached bytes* — recoverable by re-dropping the file, an order of magnitude less than losing annotations. Carrying it here would have put a schema migration in the same change as the one that stops work being lost.
  - **VERIFICATION, and where it stops.** The pure halves — key derivation, the migration decision, the prefix scan, the targeted removal — are a node suite (`src/utils/__tests__/annotationKey.test.ts`, 25 tests, no DOM), which is the cheaper instrument this repo prefers; `PEFixtureOptions` gained `timeDateStamp` because every builder wrote 0 unconditionally and **there was nothing for two builds to differ on**. The wiring is six tests appended to `src/__tests__/App.dom.test.tsx`, driving the real `App` and the real `localStorage`: remount, rename, migration (including that the legacy key is byte-for-byte untouched and that a second load with it removed still finds the record), no-migration-over-a-current-record, the two-same-named-builds case end to end, and the settings collision. **The last of those goes through the DROP handler rather than the browse input**, because `userEvent.upload` enforces the input's `accept=".exe,.dll,.sys,.ocx"` and a file named `font-size` never reaches `handleFile` that way — it is also the only test in that file that exercises `onDrop` at all. **SIX NEGATIVE CONTROLS, ALL DISCRIMINATING, NONE INERT**: dropping `timeDateStamp` from the key reddens the two-builds rows and nothing else; **removing the migration read** reddens the legacy rows while every fresh-key row stays green — the control that matters most, since a migration is exactly the thing a green suite hides; restoring the bare-name key reddens all six App rows including the settings collision; making the migration delete the legacy key reddens the two non-destructiveness rows; omitting `fileName` from the persisted record reddens two rows — and closing the gap *that* control found is why the remount row now asserts the **app** writes the name, `FileLoader.dom.test.tsx` having seeded its own records and stayed green over a persist path that omitted it; silencing the malformed-blob warning reddens its own row while its "nothing was stored" liveness half stays green. **Not verified**: no human has seen any of this in a browser, and jsdom's `localStorage` is not a quota-bearing one, so the `setItem` failure arms are reached by neither suite.
- **THE INDEXEDDB RECENT-FILE CACHE COLLIDED ON FILENAME TOO, AND THE HARD PART WAS THE MIGRATION RATHER THAN THE KEY.** (Summarised in `CLAUDE.md` under **Conventions → localStorage**, beside the annotation-key paragraph, because its key *is* that paragraph's — even though this store is IndexedDB and that section is nominally about localStorage.) `src/utils/recentFiles.ts` created its object store with `keyPath: "name"`, so `store.put({ name, buffer, … })` for `v2/setup.exe` overwrote `v1/setup.exe`'s record: the cached bytes were gone and nothing said so. `FileLoader`'s recents list joined its IndexedDB half to its annotation half on the name as well, and rendered each row with `key={f.name}`, so even had two rows existed React would have reconciled them as one. The split from `peek-a-bin-v3uh.5` was correct and is worth keeping in mind when ranking: **cached bytes are recoverable by re-dropping the file and annotations are not**, which is why one bead shipped first and this one waited. (`peek-a-bin-mtry`)
  - **ONE COMPOSITE-KEY RULE, AND THE SPLIT IS WHAT ENFORCES IT.** `annotationKeyFor` was `ANNOTATION_KEY_PREFIX + <size>-<stamp>[-<guid>]` inline; the identity half is now `buildKey(id)` and `annotationKeyFor` is `` `${ANNOTATION_KEY_PREFIX}${buildKey(id)}` ``. `recentFileKey(pe)` is `buildKey(buildIdentityOf(pe))` and nothing else. **Two identity rules that can disagree would be strictly worse than the collision either one fixes** — the recents list joins the two stores, so a build the cache calls one thing and the annotation store calls another is a join that silently misses and every row reads "no annotations". A test asserts `` `${ANNOTATION_KEY_PREFIX}${recentFileKey(pe)}` === annotationKey(pe) `` character for character; respelling one derivation's separator reddens exactly that row.
  - **THE PREFIX IS NOT CARRIED INTO THE IDB KEY.** `peek-a-bin:annotations:` exists to separate annotation records from the ~18 settings keys sharing one flat `localStorage`. A record in a database of its own has nothing to be separated from, so the IDB key is the bare identity and the prefix is applied only where a `localStorage` key is wanted.
  - **A `keyPath` CANNOT BE CHANGED IN PLACE, WHICH IS THE WHOLE MIGRATION.** The v2 `onupgradeneeded` reads every record with `getAll`, calls `deleteObjectStore` (which drops the contents), recreates the store under `keyPath: "key"`, and writes every record back. Two mechanical rules: `deleteObjectStore`/`createObjectStore` are legal **only inside a `versionchange` transaction**, so this cannot be a migration the app runs later; and that transaction **commits the moment control returns to the event loop with no request outstanding**, so the rewrite is issued from `getAll`'s own `onsuccess` — one unbroken chain, no `await`, no timer. A failure aborts and rolls back, so the worst case is "no recents this session", never a half-rekeyed store; that path is asserted by injecting a failing `put`.
  - **A v1 RECORD IS CARRIED FORWARD UNDER `name:<name>`, NOT RE-PARSED — AND THAT IS THE JUDGEMENT THE BEAD ASKED FOR.** A v1 record has a name and no build identity, and there is no way to derive one without re-parsing the bytes. The bytes *are* right there and `parsePE` *is* synchronous, so re-deriving was possible: it would mean running the PE parser up to five times, over up to 50 MB each, synchronously, inside a database upgrade, on the main thread, on records that may no longer parse at all — and a throw there aborts the `versionchange` transaction, fails the open, and leaves the user with **no recents and no way back**. The prize would have been that a migrated file keeps a build-keyed annotation join, over bytes that are recoverable by re-dropping the file. So the name is carried instead. The prefix cannot collide with a real build key, which begins with `size` and therefore with a digit. **Silently losing all five was the outcome ruled out; a stale key was not.**
  - **A LEGACY RECORD AGES OUT ON ITS OWN, AND DEDUPING IT BY NAME WAS REFUSED.** It is an ordinary record with an old `lastOpened`, so eviction takes it oldest-first. Re-opening its file therefore adds a build-keyed record *beside* it, and both rows appear briefly. Deleting the legacy row on a name match at save time would fix that — and is **exactly the name-keyed eviction this bead removed**: with two builds of one binary the wrong one would go. A legacy row joins annotations **by name**, which is all it has.
  - **EXACT-OR-NOTHING, AND THE `??` CHAIN WAS MEASURED WRONG.** `countsForRecent` reads the exact key for a build-keyed row and the name index only for a legacy one. Written first as "exact, else name", it handed a *sibling build's* count to a row with no record: two builds of `setup.exe`, one annotated with five bookmarks, and both rows advertised five. It was caught by a test written for a different reason, which is the argument for asserting on the rendered text rather than on the join.
  - **REMOVING A ROW DELETES THAT BUILD'S RECORD EXACTLY.** `removeAnnotationRecord` is the precise counterpart to `removeAnnotationsFor`; the name-keyed version would take the other build's work. The legacy bare-name blob is left behind by the precise one — it is not attributable to a build, so deleting it while removing one of two same-named builds would take data the other can still adopt. Orphaned on `loadAnnotations`' precedent.
  - **VERIFICATION, AND WHERE IT STOPS. jsdom has no IndexedDB**, so nothing here has spoken to a real one. The pure decisions (`legacyRecentKey`, `isLegacyRecentKey`, `migrateV1Record`, `recentFileKey`'s agreement with `annotationKey`, `countsForRecent`) are node tests with no DOM. The wiring runs against `src/utils/__tests__/fakeIndexedDB.ts`, the in-tree double, extended here with a persisted version, an `oldVersion` on the upgrade event, `req.transaction`, `deleteObjectStore` (which clears the records, or the migration control could not fail), and an `onsuccess` that waits for the `versionchange` transaction to settle. **What that does not establish**: that a browser's IDB agrees about upgrade ordering, that `deleteObjectStore` inside `getAll`'s `onsuccess` keeps the transaction alive there, or that a 50 MB `ArrayBuffer` survives the rekey — the double stores object references. `peek-a-bin-v2u` is where a human confirms it.
  - **CONTROLS. Seven run, six discriminating, one inert and then repaired.** (1) *Skip the `onupgradeneeded` rewrite* — the migration control the bead named: 7 rows red, and every fresh-write row stays green, because they all write their own record first. (2) *`keyPath` back to `"name"`*: 9 rows. (3) *Make `annotationKeyFor` spell the composite itself, with a different separator*: 1 row — the agreement assertion, and nothing else. (4) *Route the click and the remove by name*: 2 rows. (5) *Restore the `??` name fallback*: 1 row. (6) *`key={f.name}` on the list*: **INERT** — a duplicate React key is a `console.error`, not a render failure, and no test in the file looked. A **file-wide** `console.error` spy was added (per-owner caching means a spy installed inside a test sees a clean console) and it now reddens 5 rows. (7) *Drop the claim-by-key dedup*: 3 rows.

- **`hexPatches` IS THE ONE THING `RESET` DISCARDS THAT NOTHING PUTS BACK, AND THE ANSWER IS TWO GUARDS RATHER THAN AUTO-PERSISTENCE. The refusal is the result; the guards are what stands in for it.** `AppState.hexPatches` is a `Map<fileOffset, byte>` written by `HexView`'s grid (`PATCH_BYTE`) and read by `useDisassemblyRows` (which re-disassembles over the patched bytes) and by `HexView` itself. Every other user-authored fact in the app auto-persists: bookmarks, renames and comments go to localStorage per file, so `RESET` and a browser reload cost nothing a reopen does not restore. A patch does not, and `RESET` returns `initialState` carrying a **fresh Map** (`usePEFile.ts`). The Open button was a bare `dispatch({ type: "RESET" })`, rendered one 1px divider away from Back/Forward and titled "Load new file" — a click a user makes without deciding anything — and there was **no `beforeunload` handler anywhere in `src/`**, so the two cheapest ways to lose an afternoon's patching were both silent (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.6`).
  - **A FRAMING CORRECTION FIRST, BECAUSE THE WRONG VERSION ARGUES FOR A DIFFERENT CHANGE.** The bead was filed as "`hexPatches` is never persisted". It is: `utils/exportSchema.ts`'s `ExportSchemaV1` carries `hexPatches: [number, number][]`, `serializeState` writes it, `validateImport` validates it back (including the 0-255 range), and `AddressBar`'s Import handler rebuilds the Map and dispatches `IMPORT_FULL_ANALYSIS`. Verified in session 27 by reading all four sites. So the gap is not persistence, it is **auto**-persistence plus a guard — and the deliberate channel that does keep them sits in the same toolbar as the button that throws them away, which is why the prompt can point at it.
  - **AUTO-PERSISTING INTO THE ANNOTATION BLOB IS REFUSED, four ways, and the fourth is the one that matters.** (1) A patch is a byte of the **file**, not an annotation about it; the blob is the annotation blob. (2) The blob is per-file and quota-bounded, and its persist effect already swallows a quota throw in silence — so a patch set large enough to matter is exactly the one that would fail to save without saying so, which is worse than not saving at all. (3) The export channel exists, in the same toolbar as the button that discards them. (4) **A patch silently restored on reopen makes the file disassemble differently with no affordance saying why.** `useDisassemblyRows` re-disassembles over patched bytes, so a stale auto-restored patch is not a cosmetic annotation reappearing — it is a *different instruction stream*, presented as the file's. That is the house doctrine's own failure mode (a narrower or altered answer wearing a complete one's shape) reached from the persistence side, and no flag on a Map fixes it, because the surface that would have to say so is the disassembly listing.
  - **The guards therefore share ONE predicate, `state.hexPatches.size > 0`, in `AddressBar.tsx`.** (a) `handleReset` asks `confirm(patchLossPrompt(count))` and returns without dispatching anything on No — nothing at all, since a partial reset would be worse than either outcome. (b) A `beforeunload` listener registered **only while a patch exists**, calling `preventDefault()` and setting `returnValue` beside it for the engines that still consult the older spelling.
  - **The prompt NAMES THE COUNT AND NAMES EXPORT, and both halves are the point.** A confirm that only says "are you sure" leaves a user who answers No with nothing to do differently; the prompt is the only place in the tool that says patches are session-only, and it is the only place that points at the channel which keeps them. `patchLossPrompt` is a pure function so the count and the pluralisation ("1 byte patch", not "1 byte patches") are testable without a dialog. **`confirm` bare**, as `DisassemblyView.tsx`'s >50,000-line export prompt already does: the app has **no toast mechanism and one was not invented for a guard** — `peek-a-bin-p0tz`'s rule, established when fourteen of eighteen clipboard sites were left failing silently for exactly that reason. A dialog is the right instrument here anyway, since the guard needs an *answer* and a toast cannot take one.
  - **The unload handler deliberately supplies NO WORDING.** Every current engine ignores whatever a handler puts in `returnValue` and shows its own sentence, so prose here would be dead text that reads, to the next agent, as if it were on screen. `preventDefault()` is the entire request.
  - **The listener's condition is not defensive — an unconditional listener is its own defect.** It would make every reload of an unpatched session ask a question with no subject, which is how a guard trains a user to click through it. Keyed on the **count** rather than on the Map identity, so an unrelated `PATCH_BYTE`/`UNDO_PATCH` pair does not churn the registration, while the last `UNDO_PATCH` or `CLEAR_PATCHES` tears it down.
  - **A jsdom TRAP THAT WOULD HAVE BEEN SILENT, and it is why `confirm` is spied FILE-WIDE.** jsdom's unstubbed `window.confirm` is a "not implemented" stub: it writes to the virtual console and returns `undefined`, which is **falsy**. So the moment this guard landed, any test anywhere in the tree that clicks Open with a patch in state would stop dispatching `RESET` and would be asserting against a dialog nobody answered — green or red for the wrong reason. Checked: **no existing row in the tree clicks Open**, so nothing had to be repaired; the spy is a file-wide `beforeEach` in `AddressBar.dom.test.tsx` defaulting to `true` ("the user said go ahead") for whoever adds the next one.
  - **THREE NEGATIVE CONTROLS, ALL THREE DISCRIMINATING, none inert.** (1) Dropping `count > 0` from `handleReset` fails **exactly one** row, *does not ask at all when there is nothing to lose* — the predicted one, and the fact that it is the only one is itself the evidence that the other confirm rows are about the prompt's content rather than its existence. (2) Removing `removeEventListener` from the effect's cleanup fails **three** rows; the load-bearing one is *disarms when the last patch is cleared*, which fails **in isolation** (`-t` on that row alone) because it arms and disarms inside one test. The other two — *leaves an unpatched session alone* and *arms when the first patch arrives* — fail only through cross-test leakage, which is the same defect seen from outside and is worth knowing is order-dependent. (3) Removing the effect's `patchCount === 0` early return fails the `App.dom.test.tsx` row, which asserts a freshly opened file objects to nothing.
  - **What the tests are and are NOT evidence for.** The confirm rows read the argument the component handed `confirm`, which is the whole of what the component decides; jsdom has no dialog and performs no layout, so nothing here says the string is legible or that a user reads it. The `beforeunload` rows dispatch a **cancelable** `Event` and read `defaultPrevented` — cancelable is load-bearing, since `preventDefault()` on a non-cancelable event sets no flag and the row could never go red — and that flag is exactly the request the platform acts on; but no browser has shown the resulting dialog here, so the unload guard belongs in the `peek-a-bin-v2u` browser pass. The `App.dom.test.tsx` row drives the wiring through the **real** reducer and the **real** import handler, reaching `hexPatches` by uploading an analysis JSON to `AddressBar`'s own `input[accept=".json"]` — the honest route, since that is the channel the prompt points at, and `HexView`'s grid is out of reach in jsdom (virtualized, so `virtual-core` renders zero rows).
  - **A SEQUENCING OBLIGATION, not a rule about this code.** Any later "Close file"/`RESET` affordance — a command-palette entry, a keyboard shortcut — must route **through** `handleReset` rather than dispatching `RESET` beside it, or it reintroduces the defect with the guard sitting untouched two lines away.
- **THE COMMAND PALETTE'S "ACTION" WAS AN OPTIONAL STRING, WHICH MADE "NO ACTION" AND "AN ACTION NOBODY HANDLES" THE SAME SHAPE.** `ResultItem.action?: string` was read by one `if (item.action)` in `handleSelect`; everything else fell through to `SET_ADDRESS` + `SET_TAB` on `item.address`, which for the palette's one non-navigation entry was **0**. So a malformed entry did not fail, it navigated to address 0 in the disassembly tab, and a mistyped event name dispatched into the void — `window.dispatchEvent` fires an event nobody listens for happily and returns `true`. `ResultTarget` is a discriminated union now — `{ kind: "navigate"; address; tab } | { kind: "event"; event } | { kind: "action"; action: AppAction }` — consumed by one `switch` closed with a `never` assert, so a fourth kind fails the build rather than selecting a row that does nothing (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.10`). Six things, three of them refusals:
  - **Tab commands are DERIVED from `VIEW_TAB_LABELS`, never spelled**, and the same argument that put that map in one place applies here: it is what stops a tab being called one thing on its button, another in `analysisNotice`'s prose and a third in the palette. `VIEW_TABS` supplies the membership and the order, so a ninth tab gets a command without anyone remembering to add one. The control discriminates — spelling `"Go to Section Table"` for one tab reddens **3** rows, including the rendered one, because the test derives its expectation from the map rather than restating the label.
  - **THE CLOSED `PaletteEventName` UNION IS ONLY HALF THE RULE, AND THE OTHER HALF IS MEASURED.** A union stops a typo at the call site and nothing else: adding a member for an event no component listens for **type-checks with zero errors** (measured, by doing it — `peek-a-bin:toggle-graph` plus a command using it, `tsc --noEmit` clean). So the union is derived from an array, `PALETTE_EVENTS`, and `src/components/__tests__/paletteEvents.test.ts` reads it back against every `addEventListener("peek-a-bin:…"` in `src/` outside the test directories. It is a **node** test, not a `*.dom.test.tsx`: it renders nothing, and `import.meta.url` is not a `file:` URL under jsdom, which is the trap `build/domTestNaming.test.ts` was itself bitten by. Liveness halves beside it: the table must be non-empty, the scanned corpus must be non-empty, and at least one `peek-a-bin:` listener must be found, since a scrape fails by matching nothing.
  - **`peek-a-bin:show-xrefs` is REFUSED although it is listened for.** Its handler needs a `detail.address` and lives inside `DisassemblyView`, so from any other tab the command is a no-op — precisely the class the discriminated union was introduced to close. Pairing it with a `SET_TAB` would not fix it either: `DisassemblyView` is lazy, so an event fired in the same tick as the tab switch races its mount. **No new event was invented for anything**, which is the standing rule (`Conventions → Custom events`) and the reason several plausible commands are simply absent: Toggle graph view, Go to entry point, Show in hex and Export analysis all live as local state or a local handler inside one component with no cross-component channel, and a palette entry is not a reason to build one.
  - **`RESET` ("close file") and `CLEAR_PATCHES` are REFUSED for the opposite reason — they work.** Both discard the user's own work and both already have an entry point (`FileLoader`'s Open, `HexView`'s Clear patches button) where a confirmation belongs; a palette row a fuzzy match away is a second, unguarded path to the same loss. Sequence any confirm-guard bead **before** re-proposing either.
  - **The 15-per-category cap now admits itself, and the admission is bounded rather than exact.** The line reads `showing the first 15 matches — refine the query`, not "N more": an exact remainder means matching **every** candidate on every keystroke, and the string map of a large image holds millions of entries (`MAX_STRING_SCAN_BYTES` is 64 MiB), so the admission would cost more than the answer it qualifies. `collect()` therefore stops at the FIRST match past the cap — one extra `match` call — and records the category in a `truncated` set. Same truncation-admission class as `TRUNCATION_MARKER` (`peek-a-bin-nygv`), `ImportEntry.truncated` (`tmo9`), `ResourceTree.incomplete` (`dhcx`) and the hex search's match cap (`v3uh.7`), and like the last two it goes **on a count line, not on a row**: the cut is a property of the category's whole match list, so no row is "the incomplete one".
  - **It is a COUNT LINE and must never enter the option list.** `role="presentation"`, no `tabIndex`, no click handler — the palette is an `aria-activedescendant` listbox, so a sixteenth `role="option"` would be somewhere the arrow keys land and `activeDescendantId` would happily point at it. Asserted directly: 15 options with the notice present, the notice absent from `queryAllByRole("option")`, and 20 ArrowDowns leaving the highlight on the fifteenth row.
  - **Three arms, three separate controls, all discriminating**: no-op the `event` arm → the 2 event rows redden and every navigate row stays green; no-op the `action` arm → the 2 action rows redden and nothing else; delete the admission → the 3 over-cap rows redden while the exactly-3-matches row stays green. None inert. What is **not** covered: nothing renders the palette in a browser, so the notice's dimmed styling and its indent under a category are typecheck-and-reading only (`peek-a-bin-v2u`); and the palette still returns **nothing at all** when no file is loaded, `results` short-circuiting on a null `peFile` above the command table — so `Open Settings` is unreachable from the palette on the loader screen. Recorded as the behaviour it is rather than changed, since per-command availability is a design question and not this bead's.
- **AN EXEMPTION LIST NEEDS A THIRD DIRECTION: `DOC_ONLY_KEYS` COULD NOT SEE ITS OWN ENTRIES GOING STALE, AND THAT WAS MEASURED RATHER THAN ARGUED.** `src/components/__tests__/keyboardShortcuts.test.ts` compares `SHORTCUT_GROUPS` (the `?` panel) against `docs/keyboard.md` as key-token sets, in both directions, with `DOC_ONLY_KEYS` exempting the keys the panel deliberately omits. `peek-a-bin-w50c` already found one hole in that map — an exemption naming a key the doc had stopped documenting — and closed it with a liveness half: every entry must name a token the doc still carries. **The second hole is the mirror of the first and the liveness half cannot reach it.** An exemption only ever *skips* a doc→panel check, so the moment an exempted key acquires a panel binding, its entry starts excusing an absence that is no longer an absence — and the liveness half still passes, because the doc row it names is exactly where it always was. The entry's stated reason becomes prose that is false and that nothing can read (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.12`).
  - **THE MEASUREMENT, which is the whole of the case for the new assertion.** `DOC_ONLY_KEYS` exempted `home` and `end` "for the tablist … live only while the tab bar has focus". Binding Home/End for the disassembly listing — panel rows added, doc rows added, four bindings landed — left the **pre-existing, unmodified guard at 135/135 green**, with both exemptions still on the page still claiming the keys are panel-less. Not one assertion in the file moved. That run is the evidence; everything else here is its consequence.
  - **The repair is one more `it.each` over the same map: no `DOC_ONLY_KEYS` entry may name a token `SHORTCUT_GROUPS` binds.** It reports the binding it found (`key`, `category`, `action`) rather than only failing, because the useful next action is "delete the entry", and the message has to distinguish that from "the doc row went missing", which is the other assertion's failure. Re-adding `"home"` to the map reddens **this assertion and nothing else** — the second half of the measurement, and the reason the assertion is not redundant with any of the four already there.
  - **`"left"` and `"right"` STAY, and they are the reason the map exists rather than an oversight.** Bare arrows are deliberately absent from the `?` panel: every other row there is a **global** binding, and the unmodified arrows belong to the disassembly view everywhere outside the tab bar, so listing them would state something false about two very common keys (`peek-a-bin-w50c`'s own argument). Home/End left that population by acquiring a *global* spelling of their own — the listing binding — not by the rule changing. A future agent tidying the map to nothing has misread it.
  - **Same family as `build/guardShape.test.ts`: an instrument that judges the audit rather than the thing audited.** Both exist because a guard whose population has quietly emptied — or whose exemption has quietly stopped applying — passes by no longer looking. Three directions now: doc→panel, panel→doc, and exemption→panel. There is no fourth worth adding; an exemption naming a token neither list carries is caught by the liveness half already.
  - **The doc's own prose was a third copy of the false claim and had to move with the code.** `docs/keyboard.md`'s Tips said the four tablist keys "do nothing anywhere else", which after this stage is true of `←`/`→` alone. One token now maps to **two doc rows** — the Navigation row for the tab bar and a Disassembly row for the listing — and the guard's meaning check unions every row a token resolves to, so both scopes are described and neither can be quietly dropped.

- **THE FOUR BINDINGS THAT EXPOSED IT, and one fixture the brief asked for that CANNOT BE BUILT.** `[` / `]` (previous / next function), `Home` / `End` (first / last row of the listing) and `F3` / `Shift+F3` (next / previous search match) went into `hooks/useDisassemblyKeyboard.ts` above the arrow arm (`peek-a-bin-v3uh.12`). Walking the function list is the core loop of triage and had needed the mouse or the sidebar; nothing reached the top or bottom of a section; and the search cursor advanced only while the search **input** held focus, which is the one place `handleKeyDown` returns early. Three things are worth keeping:
  - **`sortedFuncs` is a NEW READ and therefore a new dependency**, added to `UseDisassemblyKeyboardArgs`, to the destructuring and to the array, which is now **38** entries. `hooks/__tests__/disasmHandlerDeps.test.ts` walks the AST and fails on a read without an entry *or* an entry without a read; landing the read without the entry reddens `declares sortedFuncs, which its body reads` and nothing else, which is the guard doing its job. Home/End were routed through `seekAddressableRow(rows, currentIndex, …)` partly *because* both of its reads are already declared, and F3 through the already-memoised `search` object, so neither costs a dependency.
  - **CLAMPING IS A REFUSAL, NOT A RE-DISPATCH.** A `]` on the last function does nothing rather than dispatching `sortedFuncs[last].address` — that address is *below* the cursor at every row of the last function but its first, so "clamping" by dispatching it moves the cursor backwards on a forward key. That is `seekAddressableRow`'s own stated rule for the arrows ("a downward key must never move the cursor up") one binding over. The test asserts on the **action log** as well as the address, since a state check alone passes against a handler that re-dispatches the address it already holds. `binarySearchFunc` (the one declaration of "which function contains this address") is reused rather than re-derived, but it answers `null` in the padding *between* functions — a position no index names — so there the base is the boundary itself and `]` reaches the function above the cursor while `[` reaches the one below, rather than skipping whichever one the cursor just left.
  - **THE SEPARATOR-TAILED FIXTURE IS NOT CONSTRUCTIBLE, and the control was redirected rather than tuned away.** The plan called for a listing whose LAST row is a separator, to exercise `seekAddressableRow`'s fallback from `End` — `peek-a-bin-a5sw`'s shape. `useDisassemblyRows` emits a separator after a `ret`/`retn`/`jmp`/`int3` **only when a further instruction follows** (`if (next && !funcMap.has(next.address))`), and that instruction's own row is pushed immediately after, so no listing it builds can end with one; a separator is also the only row `rowAddress` answers `null` for, and row 0 is always a label or an instruction. **The skip half of the helper is therefore structurally unreachable at either extreme**, and indexing `rows[0]` / `rows[rows.length - 1]` directly is inert against it. What *does* discriminate is the helper's other half: it answers `null` when `to === from`, so `End` on the last row dispatches nothing where a bare index re-dispatches the address the cursor already holds — invisible in `currentAddress`, visible in the action log, and the naive version reddens exactly that row. **`Home` has no matching case, and that is a fact about the rows rather than a gap**: row 0 is the first function's LABEL, which carries the same address as the instruction below it, and `binarySearchRows` resolves a shared address to the *last* row holding it, so a cursor at the first instruction sits at index 1 and `Home` is a real one-row move. The fallback itself stays covered as arithmetic in `hooks/__tests__/rowSearch.test.ts`, which already pins the trailing-separator case.

- **THE TOP TOOLBAR CLIPPED FOUR CONTROLS INTO UNREACHABILITY, AND THE HALF THAT REDIRECTS THE FIX IS THAT NOTHING IN THE ROW CAN SHRINK.** `src/components/AddressBar.tsx` was ONE flex row — `flex items-center gap-1 px-3 py-1.5 …` — holding ~20 flex items plus six dividers, with no `flex-wrap`, no `overflow-x` and no `min-w-0`. Every item in it is a single word, a fixed-size SVG or an `<input>` with a definite `width: 12rem`, so `min-width: auto` resolves to min-content == preferred width for all of them: the row cannot absorb one pixel of deficit and simply overflows. `App.tsx` gives the bar no horizontal scroll and `src/styles/index.css` sets `body { overflow: hidden }`, so the overflow was **clipped away rather than scrolled to** — Export, Import, Chat and the Settings cog went off-screen and became unclickable by any input, and for the cog that is the only *visible* route to the API-key/model settings that Chat and Enhance silently bounce to when no key is configured. It is not a tablet bug: on the arithmetic below, 1366px clips by **263px** (Export, Import, Chat and the cog entirely gone, Go cut mid-button) and 1440px by **189px**. **The corollary is what matters for anyone reading the code: adding `shrink-0` to the tail would have changed NOTHING.** What fixes it is (a) letting the row wrap, (b) removing items when narrow, (c) shortening items, and (d) giving exactly ONE item a `min-width` low enough to absorb the deficit — and (d) is what turns a cliff into continuous degradation. This is the app's **first responsive code**: there were zero `sm:`/`md:`/`lg:`/`xl:`/`2xl:` variants anywhere in `src/` before it, and `flex-wrap` itself is the **third** crowded toolbar here to answer overflow the same way (`HexView.tsx:855`, `XrefPanel.tsx:287` already carried it), so it is the house pattern rather than a new idea. Two breakpoints, both Tailwind 4 defaults with **no CSS or config change at all** — `src/styles/index.css` line 1 is a bare `@import "tailwindcss"` with no `@theme`, no `--breakpoint-*` override and no `@media` rule, and there is no `tailwind.config.*` or `postcss.config.*` in the repo: `2xl` (1536px) governs one thing, tabs inline versus on their own row, and `lg` (1024px) one thing, dividers shown versus hidden. Keep the two concerns separate rather than folding them into one breakpoint. (`peek-a-bin-cgu1`)
  - **THE ARITHMETIC IS COMPUTED AND HAS NEVER BEEN MEASURED, AND THE ONE STRUCTURAL CONSEQUENCE IS THAT NO DEFAULT BREAKPOINT IS ABOVE THE SINGLE ROW.** A 0.6em advance (JetBrains Mono is exactly 600/1000 units) at the **two** font sizes the bar mixes — the container is `text-sm` (14px → 8.4px/char) while Undo/Redo/Go/Export/Import/Chat carry their own `text-xs` (12px → 7.2px/char) — plus the Tailwind spacing scale gives: the single row as it stood **~1629px**; row 1 alone with the tabs removed **~941px**, or **845px** once the address field can shrink; the tablist alone (59 characters of label) **684px**; row 1 with the dividers hidden **~863px**, or **767px** at the address floor. So **1629 > 1536**, and `2xl:` alone would have left a ~93px band still clipping *inside the tier that is supposed to look like today* — which is exactly why the flexible address field is not optional polish. In `[1536, 1629)` the field yields up to 96px (192 → its 96px floor) so the row needs at most ~1533px; above ~1629 there is slack, the field sits at its 192px cap and the tier is identical to before. Honest cost, one sentence: in that band the address field is narrower than it was. A different fallback font moves every one of these numbers, and a wider one moves the tablist ~8% per 0.05em — which is the whole reason `flex-wrap` is the first token and the safety net: **being wrong about these estimates is a WRAP rather than a CLIP.** Two figures were corrected during the design pass and the earlier ones are stale: there are **SIX dividers at 9px of box each, not seven at 15px** (15 double-counted the flex gap, which the gap term already counts once), and the single row is ~1629px, not the ~1584px first computed.
  - **THE TABLIST IS THE CONTAINER'S FIRST ELEMENT CHILD, AND THAT IS A FOCUS-ORDER DECISION RATHER THAN A LAYOUT ONE — `order-last` IS THE WRONG WAY TO DO THIS.** The obvious implementation leaves the tablist where it was and pushes it onto its own line with `order-last basis-full … 2xl:order-none`. CSS `order` moves the **box** and not sequential focus, which every engine walks in DOM order — so below 1536px a keyboard user would tab Open → Back → Forward → Undo → Redo → **down to row 2** → **back up to row 1** → address → Export → … → cog. A WCAG 2.4.3 defect, and **invisible to this repo**: `AddressBar.dom.test.tsx`'s one-Tab-walk row asserts only that the tablist receives *exactly one* tab stop in a full walk, never where that stop falls, so `order-last` would have landed green. Moving the element to the front of the DOM instead makes visual order equal focus order in **both** tiers with no `order` class anywhere — narrow, the DOM is tabs, Open, nav, undo/redo, spacer, VA, clock, address, Go, Export, Import, Chat, cog and the visual rows are tabs / the rest, identical; wide, it is one line left to right, identical. The design pass argued the divergence was unavoidable; it is unavoidable only if the *wide* tier must keep the tabs in the middle, and that was given up deliberately. **Stated cost, accepted: in the wide one-row layout the tabs move from "after Undo/Redo" to leftmost.** Assertion A5 encodes the property, and the measurement that the pre-existing suite could not see it is in `docs/verification.md`. Its companion A4 — the tablist is a **direct** child of the element carrying `toolbar-bg` — is the guard against a future agent answering the focus-order question by splitting the bar into two real row containers, which would need two tablists and break the all-or-nothing `role="tablist"` contract immediately.
  - **`min-w-0` ON THE TABLIST IS THE LOAD-BEARING TOKEN OF THE WHOLE CHANGE, AND WITHOUT IT THE TAB ROW REPRODUCES THE TOOLBAR'S OWN DEFECT ONE LEVEL DOWN.** `basis-full` gives the strip a line to itself, but free space on that line is exactly zero, so no shrink is ever applied — and `min-width: auto` would then floor the element at its ~684px min-content, making it **overflow the container** at any width below ~708px instead of scrolling. `min-w-0` is what lets `flex-basis: 100%` actually resolve to 100%, at which point `overflow-x-auto` produces a scroller. `2xl:min-w-auto` restores the floor above the breakpoint so that in the 1536–1629px squeeze band the deficit goes into the address field and **cannot** be taken out of the tab labels; `min-w-auto` is a real utility in the installed Tailwind, verified by grepping `node_modules/tailwindcss/dist` rather than assumed from documentation. Nothing inside the tablist changed: one `role="tablist"` labelled `Views`, eight `role="tab"` children in `VIEW_TABS` order, `aria-selected`, `aria-controls`, the roving tabIndex, manual activation and the `"(N)"` titles the suite selects on all move together, because the whole block was relocated as a unit. A trap to record rather than solve: **a CSS-hidden tab still satisfies `getAllByRole("tab")` in jsdom**, `hidden` carrying no `display: none` there, so any future change that hides a tab would pass the whole suite while being wrong in a browser. No part of this change hides a tab.
  - **THE VA READOUT IS TWO SIBLING SPANS, AND THE NARROW ONE MUST NOT CARRY THE `"VA: "` PREFIX.** The padding is computed in JS (`padStart(is64 ? 16 : 8, "0")`), so no media query can reach it — hence two spans, `2xl:hidden` for the bare hex and `hidden 2xl:inline` for the labelled padded form, with `toString(16).toUpperCase()` hoisted so only the `padStart` differs. `getByText` **throws on multiple matches**, and testing-library's `getNodeText` concatenates only an element's DIRECT text-node children, so the narrow span's own text (`0x140001000`) must not match `/VA: 0x/` or the pre-existing padding assertion acquires a second match and dies. That makes adding the prefix a ready-made negative control. **The variant that does break it, so nobody writes it:** keeping ONE span and wrapping just the label in a child reddens that assertion for a reason nobody predicts — `getNodeText` then reports the outer span's own text as just the digits while the new inner span matches with `textContent` `"VA: 0x"`, so the query silently **retargets** and the `toBe` fails. The design pass recommended abandoning the whole idea on the strength of that; the two-sibling form does not have the problem. One argument recorded so it is not re-discovered as new: the design pass wanted the readout **left alone** because `StatusBar` shows RVA and file offset but never a VA, so on seven of the eight tabs the toolbar readout is the only VA on screen. That is an argument against *hiding* it, and this does not hide it — the value stays at every width, losing only the label and the leading zeros. Note also that this stage stopped being load-bearing once the address field became flexible and the dividers hid at `lg`: row 1's single-line floor is already ~767px, so its ~90px is a readability improvement rather than part of the repair.
  - **A MEASURED REFUSAL: `2xl:flex-nowrap` WAS CONSIDERED AND REJECTED, AND IT IS A JUDGEMENT RATHER THAN A CAPABILITY LIMIT.** It compiles and it would guarantee one row at the wide tier. It is refused because it would switch the `flex-wrap` safety net **off at exactly the tier whose arithmetic is least certain** — 1536 against a *computed* 1629 — and because it buys nothing: `flex-wrap` only wraps on overflow, so with the flexible address field in place the wide row does not wrap anyway. The net stays on at every width. `items-center` needs no `align-content` companion either: the container's height is auto, so there is no free cross-space for the lines to stretch into.
  - **A SECOND MEASURED REFUSAL: THE BREADCRUMBS FADE MACHINERY WAS NOT COPIED, FOR FOUR REASONS.** `overflow-x-auto` and `style={{ scrollbarWidth: "none" }}` are taken from `Breadcrumbs.tsx`; its `ResizeObserver` fade state is not. (1) The fades would be a **permanently-false constant** in every test — `domSetup.ts` installs a `ResizeObserver` whose `observe()` never fires, and the one synchronous pass reads `scrollLeft` 0 and `scrollWidth - clientWidth - 2 = -2`, so both fades compute `false` for ever; asserting them would be asserting a constant, the vacuously-green failure mode `domSetup.ts` warns about itself. (2) It would put an observer, a scroll listener, state and an effect into **the one component `CLAUDE.md` deliberately leaves without an `ErrorBoundary`** — `AddressBar` owns the global keydown handler carrying `TAB_KEYS`, so a throw there is a blank page *and* the loss of both routes to another tab. (3) `useExhaustiveDependencies` is at **error** and Breadcrumbs' companion effect needs a `biome-ignore`, so copying the pattern copies the suppression. (4) A shared hook for two callers, one of which scrolls only below 1536px, would be a third thing, and this repo's rule is that a shared declaration exists because copies **drifted** — these have not. **Stated loss:** below ~708px the tab row scrolls with no visual cue that it does. Two mitigations come free and neither was invented for the occasion: the roving tabindex already calls `.focus()`, and every engine scrolls a focused element into view, so the arrow path self-reveals; and digits 1-8 select any tab without it being visible. `scrollIntoView` on selection change was refused too — `domSetup.ts` shims it as a no-op, so any assertion would be about the shim.
  - **A THIRD MEASURED REFUSAL: THE BINARY `w-32 2xl:w-48` ADDRESS FIELD WOULD NOT HAVE CLOSED THE BAND.** The first plan switched the field between two fixed widths at the breakpoint. That leaves the 1536–1629px band clipping exactly as before, because a fixed width absorbs nothing; `flex-1 min-w-24 max-w-48` on the wrapper with `w-full` on the input is what makes the field the single deficit-absorbing item, at **every** tier rather than at one. `min-w-24` is simultaneously the 96px floor and the thing that overrides `min-width: auto`, which is what permits the shrink at all — drop it and the wrapper is floored at min-content and absorbs nothing. Two smaller refusals beside it: **Export and Import stay full-width text buttons**, because icon-only would move both accessible names from their content onto `title` attributes, a real a11y downgrade to buy width `flex-wrap` already gives free (and `patchLossPrompt` names Export as the remedy for discarding byte patches, so hiding it would point a warning at a control the user cannot see); and there is **no fourth `sm:` tier** hiding Undo/Redo, the clock and Chat, which would move the floor from ~767px to ~428px, still not reach a 375px phone, and cost exactly the three controls whose only remaining route is the command palette — where Settings and Chat already were, and that is precisely what did not help.
  - **NOTHING IN THIS BAR IS CONDITIONALLY UNMOUNTED — THE MECHANISM IS CSS, AND ONE RULE DECLARED IN ANOTHER FILE RESTS ON IT.** `hidden lg:block` leaves the element in the tree where a `{width >= 1024 && …}` conditional — what an agent reaching for measurement would write — does not, which is why assertion A8 asks for every control on a default render. Two things it protects: bare `G` focuses the address input **by ref**, so unmounting the input makes that shortcut a silent no-op; and the hidden Import `<input type="file">` is **named by hand** in `src/components/modalScaffold.ts` as the reason `focusableWithin()` filters on `offsetParent` ("permanently present, so without the filter Tab would stop on an invisible control") — relocating it or making it conditional moves the premise of a rule declared elsewhere. A measured ResizeObserver overflow check was refused for the same family of reasons: jsdom performs no layout, `src/test/browserApiStubs.ts` says in as many words that the stubs buy nothing about layout and cannot, and it deliberately refuses to fabricate a width, so a measured approach is **unverifiable in this repo by construction** — where a CSS breakpoint at least produces class strings jsdom can assert. One more fact that makes a fixed breakpoint sound here specifically: the toolbar uses fixed `text-sm`/`text-xs`, **not** `var(--mono-font-size)`, so the user's font-size preference cannot invalidate the tier boundaries the way it would for anything keyed to the listing's font size.

- **THE STATUS BAR IS THE OTHER BAR OUTSIDE `<main>`, ITS LAST CHILD IS THE ANALYSIS NOTICE, AND THE FIX IS A BREAKPOINT — BUT NOT THE ONE THE BEAD NAMED.** `src/components/StatusBar.tsx`'s root is `h-5 toolbar-bg border-t border-theme text-[10px] flex items-center px-4 … shrink-0`: no `flex-wrap`, no `overflow`, and a hard `h-5`. `AddressBar` and `StatusBar` are the only two bars outside `<main>`, in a column whose `body` is `overflow: hidden` (`src/styles/index.css:16`), so what leaves the right edge is clipped away rather than scrolled to — and the last child here is the notice at `:284-296`, the red/amber label that is the **one place this strip says the analysis failed, timed out or is partial**. `peek-a-bin-cgu1` closed the first bar; this closes the second. The repair is `hidden 2xl:inline` on the two largest fields — the instruction bytes (294px: `"15B: "` plus fifteen `XX ` groups, 49 characters, and **15 IS x86's maximum instruction length**, so that is reachable rather than theoretical) and the block extent (192px) — recovering **518px** off a **~1650px** preferred width and leaving ~1132px. They are the only two fields whose fact is on screen elsewhere: the bytes are a column of the disassembly listing under the cursor, and the block extent is drawn in the graph. **`h-5` is untouched.** (`peek-a-bin-al07`)
  - **`hidden lg:inline` — THE FORM BOTH THE BEAD AND THE BRIEF ASKED FOR — IS INERT AT EVERY WIDTH, AND THAT IS THE FINDING TO CARRY FORWARD.** `lg` hides *below* 1024px, so the two fields are SHOWN across `[1024, 1650)`, which is the entire band in which the row is over-wide and has 1366 and 1440 inside it; and below 1024px the reduced bar still wants 1132px, so it does not fit there either. There is no width at which `lg` produces a row that fits. The "~820px residual" the brief predicted is `1338 − 518` and rests on the same slip — that difference is the *reduced* bar's preferred width, but the reduction is only in force below 1024px, where it is never the operative threshold. `2xl` (1536px) is what the intent requires, and it is the same breakpoint `peek-a-bin-cgu1` chose for its own only-fits-wide decision. Under `2xl` the bands are: `>= 1650px` everything at full size; `[1536, 1650)` a mild ≤114px deficit spread over ten items; **`[1132, 1536)` clean and unshrunk, which is 1152, 1280, 1366 and 1440**; `[718, 1132)` progressive shrink; `< 718px` true horizontal overflow with the notice clipped. **Do not "correct" the token back to `lg`** — `StatusBar.dom.test.tsx` asserts `2xl:inline` by name and that no `sm|md|lg|xl` variant is present, and the control for exactly that respelling is recorded as discriminating.
  - **AND THE MECHANISM IS THE OPPOSITE OF THE TOP BAR'S: THESE FIELDS *CAN* SHRINK.** The bead asserts "nothing shrinks here either (same `min-width: auto` reasoning as cgu1's epic body)". That reasoning is sound for `AddressBar`, every item of which is a single word, a fixed-size SVG or an `<input>` with a definite width — so min-content == preferred width and the row can absorb nothing. It does not transfer: every field here is **wrappable text**, and there is no `whitespace-nowrap` in `StatusBar.tsx` or anywhere in `src/styles/`, so `min-width: auto` resolves to the longest word — 24px (`15B:`) for the 294px byte field, 48px (`function`) for the notice. So above ~840px the pre-fix bar did not clip: it **shrank**, the notice keeping its box on the line while its text wrapped to two ~12px lines in a 48px column, and the byte field wrapping to ~15 lines inside a 20px `items-center` box whose overflow is `visible` — ~80px spilling above, over `<main>`, and ~80px below, off the viewport. Both readings agree the row does not fit and that the notice is the casualty; they disagree about what 1366px looks like, and only the corrected one explains why `lg` cannot help. **One practical consequence: `shrink-0` on these fields would be actively harmful here** — it would convert the wrap into the clean horizontal clip the fix exists to prevent, where at the top bar the same token was merely inert.
  - **THREE REFUSALS.** (1) `h-auto min-h-5` + `flex-wrap` is the direct analogue of the top bar's repair and would be a complete fix at every width; it is refused because it makes the strip two lines on every narrow window and so changes the app's vertical budget, a larger judgement than this defect needs. (2) Reordering so the notice is not last: its position is a recorded decision — the `(partial)` marker sits *beside the count* on purpose, and the comment at `:268-270` says why — so moving it is its own judgement, which is why the fourth new assertion pins "last" rather than letting a reorder land green. (3) Expecting anything to absorb the deficit, per the correction above.
  - **THE VA FIELD IS A ZERO-LOSS OPPORTUNITY TAKEN WHILE IN THE FILE, AND IT UNBLOCKS SOMETHING ELSE.** The bar showed the RVA and the file offset and **never the virtual address**, so on seven of the eight tabs `AddressBar`'s readout was the only VA on screen — which is exactly the argument that stopped `peek-a-bin-cgu1.4` from HIDING that readout and left it merely shortened. `VA:` now sits ahead of `RVA:` and `File:`, most-absolute-first, for ~90px of content plus its 16px margin, which is counted in the ~1650px above. With the value here, dropping the toolbar's copy becomes free at every width. It is the one genuinely behavioural assertion in the new suite, derived from the fixture's own image base rather than hardcoded.
  - **EVERY FIGURE IS COMPUTED AND NOTHING HAS BEEN SEEN IN A BROWSER.** 6px per character from a 0.6em advance at `text-[10px]`, plus the Tailwind spacing scale; `lg` = 64rem and `2xl` = 96rem read out of the installed `tailwindcss/theme.css`. Tailwind is not loaded under vitest and jsdom performs no layout, so the four new tests are class-string and text contracts: nothing has seen a field hide, the notice be on screen, or the spill. Eight negative controls, **all eight discriminating and none inert**; the full ledger is in `docs/verification.md`. The width sweep is appended to `peek-a-bin-v2u`.

- Capstone WASM is cached in IndexedDB (`peek-a-bin-wasm`). First load fetches, subsequent loads read from cache.
- **A PERSISTED PIXEL SIZE MUST BE VALIDATED AGAINST THE VIEWPORT AND NOT ONLY AGAINST A COMPILE-TIME CONSTANT — and the derived answer must never be written back.** Two sites held the same defect: `Sidebar.tsx`'s width (`[MIN_WIDTH 180, MAX_WIDTH 400]`, `peek-a-bin:sidebar-width`) and `BottomPanelContainer.tsx`'s docked height (`[MIN_HEIGHT 80, MAX_HEIGHT 600]`, `peek-a-bin:bottom-panel-height`). Each loader validated a stored value against its own two constants and nothing else, so a size chosen on a large display was restored **verbatim** into whatever window opened next, and neither re-derived on a resize: 400px of sidebar in a 500px window leaves 100px for the whole disassembly pane, and a stored 600px docked panel is *taller than* a 400px-tall window it sits in. `components/persistedSizeClamp.ts` is the one declaration of the shared question — how large may a persisted, user-chosen size be, given the viewport — and it is DELIBERATELY a different function from `floatingClamp.ts`'s, which owns the neighbouring question and takes no size at all because it anchors on a constant slice of a drag handle. Three rules, each of which fails silently if undone:
  - **DERIVED FOR RENDERING, NEVER WRITTEN BACK.** The state and localStorage hold what the user asked for; the clamp's answer goes into an inline style and nowhere else, so restoring a large window restores the large sidebar. This is the mistake `floatingClamp`'s position made once and had fixed — a callback that spread the derived object back into `poppedOut` "replaces the user's stored position with the picture of it" — repeated one question over. The control is precise: persisting the clamped value reddens **exactly the 3** derive-don't-store rows and nothing else. Note an asymmetry in how well it is caught. The sidebar persists from a `useEffect` keyed on the width, so a mount into a small window would immediately overwrite the preference and both its rows see it; the panel persists only from `ResizeHandle`'s `onResizeEnd`, so **only its drag row can see a write-back** and its "leaves the stored height alone" row stayed green under that control. That is a property of the code rather than a hole in the audit — there is no other write path to catch.
  - **THE FLOOR WINS WHEN THE FLOOR AND THE VIEWPORT CANNOT BOTH BE SATISFIED**, `Math.max(min, …)` outermost, as a named and tested case rather than an accident of ordering. They genuinely cannot: a 180px minimum sidebar beside a 306px reserve does not fit in a 375px viewport. The argument is `floatingClamp`'s docstring's, one question over — yielding to the viewport drives the size to `viewport - reserve`, which reaches zero and then goes **negative**, leaving an element of no extent that cannot be dragged or grabbed back, where a floor that overflows is recoverable because the sidebar has a collapse rail and the panel a close button.
  - **A DRAG IS NOT CLAMPED, which is the deliberate asymmetry with `floatingClamp`, whose write IS.** There, a pointer that ran off to (5000, 5000) named a position the user did not choose. A size is different: the number a drag produces is already bounded by the site's own `MIN`/`MAX`, and clamping the write against the viewport would mean **one drag in a narrow window permanently discards a wide preference** — the exact trap the first rule exists to prevent. The visible consequence is that on a viewport too small to grant it, the element stops following the pointer before the pointer stops moving, which is what `MAX_WIDTH` already does at 400px.
  - **THE RESERVES ARE SUMS OF READ ELEMENT EXTENTS, NOT INVENTED NUMBERS, and the judgements in them are stated at the declaration.** 306px of width from `.disasm-grid.hide-bytes`' 28.5ch of fixed columns (`--col-badge 2.5ch` + `--col-addr 18ch` + `--col-mnemonic 8ch`) at the 16px maximum mono size plus `--row-px` on both sides; 166px of height from `AddressBar` at two rows (73 — the tier `16f37df` created below 1536px, and the tier that is taken, since a short window is likely there) + the section-header bar (31) + breadcrumbs (21) + the status bar (21) + one `--row-height` listing row (20). Three judgements sit inside the 306 (that the *bytes-hidden* column set is the floor rather than the full 56.5ch, which the listing scrolls horizontally rather than requiring; the 0.6em monospace advance, a property of `--font-mono`; and taking the 16px ceiling rather than the 12px default) and exactly one inside the 166 (the listing floor).
  - **THE LISTING FLOOR IS ONE ROW, AND THAT IS A BLOCKED ALTERNATIVE RATHER THAN A PREFERENCE.** Four or five rows is the size that makes the pane worth looking at, and measured by bisection, any reserve above **168** reddens `BottomPanels.dom.test.tsx`'s `it("clamps to the minimum and the maximum")`, which asserts a rendered `600px` at jsdom's 768 — i.e. it pins the constants-only clamp this module exists to widen, the repo's own recurring "the suite had pinned the defect as the rule" shape. Raising the floor is therefore a decision about that test and was reported rather than taken. The width side had 318px of headroom (boundary 624/625) and was never constrained by anything. Eleven negative controls, **none inert**; one row (idempotence) is reddened by no perturbation of the function alone and is kept as documentation, exactly as `floatingClamp.test.ts` keeps its own. **jsdom performs no layout, so nothing here is verified as layout** — the arithmetic and the wiring are, and nothing has seen a sidebar be too wide or a panel overflow its column. Full figures in `docs/verification.md` (`peek-a-bin-0tt6`).
- **`fold.ts` has a `castTypeSize` helper** for double-cast removal. Uses regex to extract bit width from type strings like `int32_t`.
- **`cleanup.ts`** runs after `structureCFG`, before `inferTypes`. Guard clause flattening is single-level only (not recursive inversion).
- **`StructRegistry`** persists across decompilation calls in the worker — don't clear it between functions in the same session.
